    Prove \cref{lem:PolySlowerThanExp}.
  \end{exercise}

  \recommended
  \begin{exercise}
  What is the connection between computable functions and 
  feasibly computable functions?
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item Show that there is a computable function whose output values
   grow at a rate that is $\bigOh(1)$, one whose values grow at
   a rate that is $\bigOh(n)$, one for $\bigOh(n^2)$, etc.
  \item The \HP{} function $K$ is uncomputable.
    Give a $\bigOh$ bound on the rate of growth of its output.
  \item Produce a function that is not computable because its output 
   values are larger than those of any computable function.
   (You need not show that the rate of growth is strictly larger, only
   that the output values are larger.)
  \end{exparts*}
  \begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item The function $f_0(x)=0$ is clearly computable and has
    output values that grow at a rate that is $\bigOh(1)$.
    Similarly, the output values of $f_1(x)=x$ grow
    at a rate that is $\bigOh(n)$,
    and $f_2(x)=x^2$ is $\bigOh(n^2)$, etc.
  \item The function
    \begin{equation*}
      K(x)=
      \begin{cases}
        0  &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
        1  &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\diverges$}  
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is bounded, so it is $\bigOh(1)$.
  \item Let $M(n)$ be the maximum value of $\TMfcn_i(j)$ where $0\leq i,j\leq n$
    and $\TMfcn_i(j)\converges$ (if there is no such maximum because none of 
    the $\TMfcn_i(j)$ converge then define $M(n)=0$).
    This is obviously not a computable function but it is a function.
    Then the function $\hat{M}(n)=M(n)+1$ has the property that for any
    computable function $\TMfcn_e$, 
    for all $n>e$ if $\TMfcn_e(n)\converges$ then $\hat{M}(n)>\TMfcn_e(n)$. 
  \end{exparts}
  \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise}
    Verify these clauses of \cref{lem:BigOhReflexiveAndTransitive}.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item The big-$\bigOh$ relation is reflexive.
    \item It is also transitive.
    \end{exparts}
  \end{exercise}

  \begin{exercise}
    Verify \cref{lem:BigThetaEquivalence}.
    \textit{Hint:} the prior exercise does some of the work.
  \end{exercise}

  \begin{exercise}
    Verify these clauses of \cref{lem:LHBigO}.
    Assume that $f,g$ are natural number functions and suppose that 
    $\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)/g(n)$ exists and equals $L\in\R\cup\set{\infty}$.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item If $L=\infty$ then $f$ grows faster than~$g$.
    \item If $L=0$ then $g$ grows faster than~$f$
    \item If $L$ is between $0$ and~$\infty$
      then the two functions have the same growth rate.
    \end{exparts}
  \end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise}
    Show that $\bigOh(2^n)\neq\bigOh(3^n)$.
  \end{exercise}  
\end{exercises}
\index{big O@big $\bigOh$|)}






% =============================================
\section{A problem miscellany}\index{problem miscellany|(}
Much of the present work in the Theory of Computation is driven by problems.
% We have already discussed some problems, notably the \HP{}
% and ones related to it.
% 
Often these come from a field outside the subject and are taken
up by computation researchers who
try to classify problems by their computational difficulty.
Here we will list some problems, to get a sense of the ones that 
appear in the subject and also to use for examples and exercises.
These are all well known in the field.









\subsection{Problems with stories}
We start with a few problems that come with stories.
Besides being fun, these stories
also give a sense of where problems come from.

\margingraphic[l]{0.18\textwidth}{complexity/pix/hamilton.png}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rowan_Hamilton}{William Rowan Hamilton 1805--1865}}\index{Hamilton, W R!picture} % By William_Rowan_Hamilton_portrait_oval.png: Unknown William_Rowan_Hamilton_portrait_oval_2.png: Unknown derivative work: Quibik [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
W~R Hamilton was a polymath whose genius was recognized early and he was 
given a sinecure as Astronomer Royal of Ireland.  
He made important contributions to classical mechanics, where his 
reformulation of Newtonian mechanics is now called Hamiltonian mechanics. 
Other work of his in physics helped develop classical field theories such as 
electromagnetism and laid the groud work for
the development of quantum mechanics.  
In mathematics, he is best known as the
\citetext{inventor of the quaternion number system}{See \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_quaternions}.}.

One of his ventures was a game, \citetext{\textit{Around the World}}{% 
  Another version was
  called \textit{The Icosian Game}.
  See \url{http://puzzlemuseum.com/month/picm02/200207icosian.htm}.}.
As below,
the vertices were holes labelled with the names of world cities.
Players put pegs in the holes, looking for a circuit that visited each 
city once and only once. 
\begin{animatedfigure}{Hamilton's \textit{Around the World} game}\centering
  % \includegraphics[height=0.75in]{complexity/icosehedron.png}
  % \hspace{2em}
  \animategraphics[poster=first]{2}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{13}{14}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\noindent It did not make Hamilton rich.
But it did get him associated with a great problem.

%<*problem:HamiltonianProblem>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}]\index{Hamiltonian Circuit@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}
Given a graph, decide if it contains a \definend{Hamiltonian circuit},
a cyclic path that includes each vertex once and only once.
\end{problem}
%</problem:HamiltonianProblem>

As stated, this is a decision problem, to
say `yes' or `no' whether the input graph has a circuit.
A related decision problem is to input a pair and decide
if it is a member of
$\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},C}\suchthat\text{$C$ is a circuit of $\mathcal{G}$}}$.
Some of the other problems described in this section are stated as
decision problems but some are other types.
We will expand on problem types in the next section. 

%<*discussion:KnightsTouri>
A special case of \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}
is the \definend{\probname{Knight's Tour}},\index{Knight's Tour@\probname{Knight's Tour} problem}
to use a chess knight to make a circuit of the squares on the board.
(Recall that a knight moves three squares at a time,
two in one direction and then one perpendicular to that direction.)
%</discussion:KnightsTouri>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1in]{complexity/pix/knight.jpg}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.2in]{complexity/pix/knightstour.png}}
\end{center}
%<*discussion:KnightsTourii>
\citetext{This is the solution given by L~Euler}{%
  The figure is from \autocite{EulerKnightsTour}.}.
In graph terms, there are sixty four vertices,
representing the board squares.
An edge goes between two vertices if they are connected by a single knight move.
\probname{Knight's Tour} asks for a \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} 
of that graph.
%</discussion:KnightsTourii>

\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} has another famous variant.

%<*problem:TravellingSalesman>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Travelling Salesman}]\index{Travelling Salesman problem@\probname{Travelling Salesman} problem}  \label{ex:TravellingSalesman}
Given a set $S=\set{c_0,\ldots c_{k-1}}$ whose elements are called
cities, and an array of integer distances $d(c_i,c_j)\in\N^+$ for all
$c_i\neq c_j$ (subject to $d(c_i,c_2)=d(c_j,c_i)$), 
\citetext{find the cheapest circuit that visits every city}{%
  \probname{Travelling Salesman} was first posed by K~Menger,
  in an article that appeared in the same journal issue as
  did G\"odel's Incompleteness Theorem.}.
\end{problem}
%</problem:TravellingSalesman>

\margingraphic[l]{0.375\textwidth}{complexity/pix/xkcdtsp.png}{\label{fig:xkcdTravellingSalesman}\href{https://xkcd.com/399/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
An instance starts with a map of the  
forty eight contiguous US~states, including the state capitals
along with the distances between them: Montpelier~VT to	Albany~NY is $254$
kilometers, etc.
From among all trips that visit each city and return back to the start,
such as 
$\text{Montpelier}\rightarrow\text{Albany}\rightarrow\text{Harrisburg}\rightarrow \cdots \rightarrow\text{Montpelier}$, we want the shortest one.

As stated, this is an optimization problem,
but we can recast it as a decision problem.
Introduce a bound $B\in\N$ and change the problem statement to
`decide if
there is a circuit less than~$B$ long'.
If we had an algorithm to quickly solve this decision problem
then we could solve the optimization problem:~ask 
whether there is a trip bounded by length $B=1$, then ask if there is a 
trip of length $B=2$, etc.
When we eventually get a `yes', we know the shortest trip.

\margingraphic{0.18\textwidth}{complexity/pix/euler.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonhard_Euler}{Leonhard Euler 1707--1783}}\index{Euler, L!picture} % Jakob Emanuel Handmann [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3ALeonhard_Euler.jpg
The next problem sounds much like \probname{Hamilton Circuit}, in that it
involves walking around a graph, exhaustively.
But it turns out to act very differently.

Today the city of Kaliningrad is in a Russian exclave between
Poland and Lithuania.
But in 1727 it was Prussian and was called 
K\"onigsberg.\index{Konigsberg@K\"onigsberg}
The Pregel river divides the city into four areas,
connected by seven bridges.
The citizens used to promenade,
to take leisurely walks or drives where they could see and be seen.
Among these citizens the question arose: can a person's walk cross each bridge
once and only once,
and arrive back at the start?
No one could think of a way but no one could think of a reason that there was
no way.
A local mayor wrote to Euler, who proved that
no circuit is possible.
This paper founded Graph Theory.
\begin{center}  \setlength{\fboxsep}{0pt} \setlength{\fboxrule}{0.8pt}
  % By Merian-Erben [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
  % https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AImage-Koenigsberg%2C_Map_by_Merian-Erben_1652.jpg
  % \autocite{EulerBridges}
  \vcenteredhbox{\fcolorbox{boldcolor}{white}{\includegraphics[height=1.2in]{complexity/pix/koenigsberg.jpg}}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=0.6in]{complexity/pix/eulerbridges.jpg}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems15.pdf}}
\end{center}
Euler's summary sketch is in the middle and the graph is on the right.

%<*prob:EulerCircuit>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Euler Circuit}]\index{Euler Circuit@\probname{Euler Circuit} problem} \label{prob:EulerCircuit}
Given a graph, find a circuit that traverses each edge once
and only once.
\end{problem}
%</prob:EulerCircuit>

Next is a problem that sounds like the prior ones, hard.
But all of us see it solved every day.

%<*prob:ShortestPath>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Shortest Path}]\index{Shortest Path@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} \label{prob:ShortestPath}
Given a weighted graph and two vertices, 
find the shortest path between them.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ShortestPath>

\noindent For instance, with this graph we could look for 
the cheapest path from $A$ to~$F$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs08.pdf}
\end{center} 
This is the problem that you have when you ask your phone for the
shortest-time
driving directions to a distant city.
There is an algorithm that solves it quickly.\footnote{%
  Dijkstra's algorithm is at worst quadratic in the number of vertices.}

\margingraphic[l]{0.2\textwidth}{complexity/pix/demorgan.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus_De_Morgan}{Augustus De Morgan 1806--1871}}\index{De Morgan, A!picture} % By Sophia Elizabeth De Morgan (Memoir of Augustus De Morgan) [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2c/De_Morgan_Augustus.jpg
The next problem was discovered in 1852 by a young mathematician, F~Guthrie,
who was coloring a map of the counties of England.
He wanted to make adjacent counties different colors and
he wanted to use as few colors as possible.
His map required only four colors and he
conjectured that for any map at all, four colors suffice.

Guthrie imposed the condition that the map's regions must be contiguous,
and he defined `adjacent' to mean sharing a border that is an interval,
not just a point; see \cref{exer:FiveColors}.
On the right below is a graph version of the problem.
Counties are represented as vertices and edges connect them if they
are adjacent.
Because this graph arises from a map, it is \definend{planar}\Dash
we can draw it in the plane so that its edges do not cross.
So \probname{Four Color}\index{Four Color@\probname{Four Color} problem} 
inputs a planar graph and tries
separate the vertices into no more than four categories, the colors,
such that adjacent vertices are in different categories.
\begin{animatedfigure}{\protect\citetext{Counties of England and the derived planar graph}{%
    This is the map today.
    The map at the time had some counties that were non-contiguous.}}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\animategraphics[height=1.5in,poster=last]{2}{languages/counties}{00}{01}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.5in]{complexity/asy/problems/problems16.pdf}}
\end{animatedfigure} 

\margingraphic[l]{0.21\textwidth}{complexity/pix/fourcolorssuffice.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_color_theorem}{Appel and Haken's post office celebrating}} % from http://mathyear2013.blogspot.com/2013/04/dr-kenneth-appel-and-four-color-map.html
% \margingraphic[l]{0.2\textwidth}{complexity/pix/fourcolorssuffice.jpg}{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_color_theorem}{Postmark celebrating the solution}} % from http://mathyear2013.blogspot.com/2013/04/dr-kenneth-appel-and-four-color-map.html
Guthrie consulted his former professor, A~De~Morgan,
who was also unable to either prove or disprove the conjecture.
But he did make the problem famous by promoting it among his
mathematical friends, including Hamilton.
It remained unsolved until 1976 when
K~Appel and W~Haken announced 
that they reduced the proof to 1\,936 cases, and gotten a
computer to check those cases.
This was the first major proof that was done on a computer and it was
controversial, although now computer work is routine.

%<*prob:GraphColorability>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Graph Colorability}]\index{Graph Colorability@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem} \label{prob:GraphColorability}
Given a graph and a number $k\in\N$, decide whether the 
graph is \definend{$k$-colorable}, 
whether we can partition its vertices into $k$-many parts 
$\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{C}_0\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{C}_{k-1}$ such
that no two same-part vertices are connected by an edge.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[\probname{Chromatic Number}]\index{Chromatic Number@\probname{Chromatic Number} problem} \label{prob:ChromaticNumber}
Given a graph, find the smallest number $k\in\N$ such that the 
graph is \definend{$k$-colorable}.
\end{problem}
%</prob:GraphColorability>

\margingraphic{0.2\textwidth}{complexity/pix/boole.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Boole}{George Boole 1815--1864}}\index{Boole, G!picture} % See page for author [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AGeorge_Boole_color.jpg
We close with a problem that 
will serve as a benchmark to which we compare other problems.
In 1847 by G~Boole outlined the subject that we today call Boolean algebra in
\citetext{\textit{An Investigation of the Laws of Thought on Which are Founded the Mathematical Theories of Logic and Probabilities}}{%
  See \protect\url{https://www.3quarksdaily.com/3quarksdaily/2018/02/george-boole-and-the-calculus-of-thought-5.html}}.
It provided a systematic mathematical formalism
for simple logical relations.

A variable is \definend{Boolean}\index{boolean}\index{boolean!variable} 
if the values it takes on are
$T$ or~$F$.
A \definend{Boolean function}\index{boolean!function}\index{function!boolean} 
inputs and outputs tuples of those. 
\definend{Boolean formulas}\index{boolean!formula} 
connect variables using the
binary \definend{and operator~$\wedge$},
the binary \definend{or operator~$\vee$},
and the unary \definend{not operator~$\neg$}.
Here is a formula with three variables.
\begin{equation*}
  f(P,Q,R)=(P\vee Q)\wedge (P\vee \neg Q)\wedge (\neg P\vee Q)
  \wedge (\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R) % \wedge (\neg P\vee R)
\end{equation*}
We will assume that the formulas are in 
\citetext{\definend{conjunctive normal form}}{%
  Any Boolean function can be expressed by a formula in that form.},\index{conjunctive normal form}\index{CNF, Conjunctive Normal Form}
so they consist of clauses of $\vee$'s connected with $\wedge$'s.
A Boolean formula is \definend{satisfiable}\index{satisfiable} 
if there is some combination
of input $T$'s and $F$'s so that the formula evaluates to~$T$.
This \definend{truth table}\index{truth table} 
shows the input-output behavior of the
function defined by that formula.
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\vee Q$ &$P\vee \neg Q$ &$\neg P\vee Q$ &$\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R$ &$f(P,Q,R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
That $T$ in the final column witnesses that this formula is satisfiable.

%<*prob:SAT>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Satisfiability}, $\SAT$]\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}\index{SAT@$\SAT$|see {Satisfiability}}  \label{prob:SAT}
Given a propositional logic formula,
decide if it is satisfiable.
\end{problem}
%</prob:SAT>

%<*prob:ThreeSAT>
\begin{problem}[\probname{$3$-Satisfiability}]\index{3 Satisfiability@\probname{$3$-Satisfiability} problem}\index{3 SAT@$3$-$\SAT$}
Given a propositional logic formula in conjunctive normal form
in which each clause has at most three variables,
decide if it is satisfiable.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ThreeSAT>

Observe that the number of rows in the truth table
is $2^v$ where $v$ is the number of input variables.
So solving this problem appears to take
exponential time.
Whether that appearance is right is a very important problem, as we will see in
later sections.







% =========================================
\subsection{More problems}
We will list some more problems and examples, albeit without stories. 

%<*prob:reachability>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}]\index{Vertex-to-Vertex Path@\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem}\label{prob:vertextovertexpath}
Given a graph and two vertices,
find if the second is reachable\index{reachable vertex}\index{vertex!reachable} 
from the first,
that is, if there is a path from the first to the second.\footnotemark[1]
\end{problem}\footnotetext[1]{There are lots of problems about paths, so 
  calling this just the \probname{Path} problem is confusing.
  Some authors call this 
  \probname{$st$-Path},\protect\index{st Path@$st$-Path problem|see {Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem}} 
  \probname{$st$-Connectivity},\protect\index{st Connectivity@$st$-Connectivity problem|see {Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem}}
  or \probname{STCON}.\protect\index{stcon@STCON problem|see {Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem}}}
%</prob:reachability>

\begin{example}
These are two Western-tradition constellations, Ursa Minor
and Draco.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems26.pdf}
\end{center}
Here we can solve the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem by eye.
For any two vertices in Ursa Minor there is a path and
for any two vertices in Draco there is a path.
But if one vertex is in the one and the other is in the other 
then there is no path. 
\end{example}

%<*prob:minspanningtree>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Minimum Spanning Tree}]\index{Minimum Spanning Tree@\probname{Minimum Spanning Tree} problem} \label{prob:minspanningtree}
Given an edge-weighted undirected graph,
find a \definend{minimum spanning tree}, a subgraph containing
all the vertices of the original graph such that its edges have a mininimum
total.
\end{problem}
%</prob:minspanningtree>

In this graph all of the edges have weights.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity21.pdf}
\end{center}
The highlighted subgraph includes all of
the vertices, that is, it 
\definend{spans}\index{span a graph}\index{spanning subgraph}\index{graph!spanning subgraph} 
the graph.
A \definend{tree}\index{tree}\index{graph!tree} 
is a graph with no cycles.
This subgraph's edge weights total to a minimum from among all
spanning subgraphs so it must not have any cycles, or else we could eliminate
an edge from the cycle and thereby lower the edge weight total without
dropping any vertices.

\probname{Mimimum Spanning Tree}\index{Mimimum Spanning Tree@\probname{Mimimum Spanning Tree} problem} 
looks like \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}
in requiring that the subgraph contain all the vertices.
However, for this problem we know algorithms that are quick, $\bigOh(n\lg n)$.

%<*prob:VertexCover>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Vertex Cover}]\index{Vertex Cover@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a 
\definend{$B$-vertex cover},\index{vertex cover}\index{graph!vertex cover}
a size~$B$ set of vertices~$C$
such that for any edge $v_iv_j$, at least one of its ends
is a member of~$C$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:VertexCover>

\begin{example}
A museum wants to post guards to watch their paintings.
They have eight halls, laid out as below and they plan to
post guards at corners.
What is the smallest number of guards that will suffice to watch
all of the hallways?
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs15.pdf}
\end{center}
Obviously, one guard will not do.
A two-element list that covers is $C=\set{w_0,w_4}$.
\end{example}

%<*prob:Clique>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Clique}]\index{Clique@\probname{Clique} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a 
\definend{$B$-clique},\index{clique in a graph}\index{graph!clique}
a set of $B$-many vertices such that any two are connected.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Clique>

If the graph nodes represent people
and the edges connect friends then we want to know if there are
$B$-many mutual friends.
Any graph with a $4$-clique has the subgraph like the one below on the left
and any graph with a $5$~clique has the subgraph like the one the right.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity02.pdf}
  \hspace{3em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity12.pdf}
\end{center}

\begin{example}
Decide if this graph has a $4$-clique.
\begin{animatedfigure}{Instance of the Clique
  problem}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=first]{1}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{08}{09}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\end{example}

%<*prob:Broadcast>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Broadcast}]\index{Broadcast@\probname{Broadcast} problem}  \label{prob:broadcast}
Given a graph with initial vertex~$v_0$, and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if you can broadcast a message from $v_0$ to every other vertex
within $B$~steps.
At each step, any node that has heard the message can transmit it to
at most one adjacent node.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Broadcast>

\begin{animatedfigure}{Instance of the Broadcast
  problem}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=first]{1}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{02}{06}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\begin{example} \label{ex:broadcast}
In the graph no vertex is more than three edges away from the initial one.
The animation shows it taking four steps to broadcast.
\end{example}

%<*prob:ThreeDMatching>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Three-dimensional Matching}]\index{Three-dimensional Matching@\probname{Three-dimensional Matching} problem}  
\label{Prob:ThreeDMatching}
Given as input a set $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$, where the sets $X,Y,Z$
all have the same number of elements, $n$,
decide if there is a \definend{matching},\index{matching, three dimensional}
a set $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ containing~$n$ elements such that no
two of the triples in $\hat{M}$ agree on any of their coordinates.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ThreeDMatching>

\begin{example}
Let $X=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$, $Y=\set{\str{b},\str{c}}$,
and~$Z=\set{\str{a},\str{d}}$, so that $n=2$.
Then $M=X\times Y\times Z$ contains these eight elements $\sequence{x,y,z}$.
\begin{center} \small
\begin{tabular}{cc}
  \multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{a}$}
  &\multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{d}$}    \\[-0.5ex]
  \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{b}$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{c}$}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    \rule{0pt}{9pt} $x=\str{a}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{a}}$  \\
    $x=\str{b}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{a}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  &\begin{tabular}{r|cc}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{b}$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{c}$}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    \rule{0pt}{9pt}  $x=\str{a}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{d}}$  \\
    $x=\str{b}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Then the set $\hat{M}=\set{\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}},
\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}}$
has the requisite $n=2$ elements and those two agree in none of their
three coordinates.
\end{example}

%<*prob:SubsetSum>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Subset Sum}]\index{Subset Sum@\probname{Subset Sum} problem}  \label{prob:SubsetSumDecision}
Given a multiset of natural
numbers $S=\set{n_0,\,\ldots\, n_{k-1}}$ and a target $T\in\N$,
decide if a subset of~$S$ sums to the target.
\end{problem}
%</prob:SubsetSum>

Recall that a multiset\index{multiset} 
is like a set in that the order of the elements
is not significant but is different than a set in that repeats do not
collapse:~the multiset $\set{1,2,2,3}$ is different than the 
multiset $\set{1,2,3}$.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:SubsetSum}
Decide if there is a subset of
$\set{911,22,821,563,405,986,165,732}$ that adds to $T=1173$.
(This works: $\set{165, 986, 22}$.)
% sage: [randint(1, 999) for i in range(8)]
% [911, 22, 821, 563, 405, 986, 165, 732]
% \begin{equation*}
% 911
% \quad 22
% \quad 821
% \quad 563
% \quad 405
% \quad 986
% \quad 165
% \quad 732
% \end{equation*}
\end{example}

%<*prob:Knapsack>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Knapsack}]\index{Knapsack@\probname{Knapsack} problem}
Given a finite set $S$ whose elements~$s$
have a weight~$w(s)\in\N^+$ and a value~$v(s)\in\N^+$, along with a
weight bound $B\in\N^+$ and a value target~$T\in\N^+$, find a subset
$\hat{S}\subseteq S$ whose elements have a total weight less than or equal
to the
bound and total value greater than or equal to the target.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Knapsack>

Imagine that we have items to pack in a knapsack and we can carry at most
ten pounds.
Can we pack a value of~$T=100$ or more?
\begin{center}\small 
\begin{tabular}{r|cccc}
  \tabulartext{Item}
    &\tabulartext{a} &\tabulartext{b} &\tabulartext{c} &\tabulartext{d}  \\
  \cline{2-5}
  \rule{0pt}{9pt}  % scooch more room
  \tabulartext{Weight}  &$3$  &$4$  &$5$   &$6$  \\
  \tabulartext{Value}   &$50$ &$40$ &$10$  &$30$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
We pack the most value while keeping
to the weight limit by taking items (a) and~(b).
So we cannot meet the value target.

%<*prob:Partition>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Partition}]\index{Partition@\probname{Partition} problem}
Given a finite multiset~$A$ that has for each of its elements
an associated positive number size $s(a)\in\N^+$,
decide if there is a division of the set into two halves,
$\hat{A}$ and $A-\hat{A}$, so that the total of the sizes is the same in
both halves, $\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}s(a)=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}s(a)$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Partition>

\begin{example}
The set
$A=\set{\str{I},\str{a},\str{my},\str{go},\str{rivers},\str{cat},\str{hotel},\str{comb}}$
has eight words.
The size of a word is the number of letters.
Then $\hat{A}=\set{\str{cat},\str{river},\str{I},\str{a},\str{go}}$
gives $\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}s(a)=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}s(a)=12$.
\end{example}


% Recall that if we have an exponential relation $b^n=a$ then we say that
% the exponent~$n$ is the logarithm, base~$b$, of~$a$, written $\log_b(a)=n$.
% For instance, $2^7=128$ so we say that $\log_{2}(128)=\lg(128)=7$.

% Discrete logarithms are the discrete analogue of those.
% Fix a prime~$p$ and consider the numbers $G=\set{0,1,\ldots\, p-1}$.
% We can multiply two of these and then take the answer module~$p$.


% \begin{problem}[Discrete Logarithm]
% Let $p\in\N$i be a prime.
% Given a base $b\in\set{0,1,\ldots\,p-1}$ and
% a number $a\in\set{0,1,\ldots\,p-1}$, find an exponent~$n$ such that
% $b^n\equiv a\pmod{p}$.
% \end{problem}

% But that is slow.

%<*prob:Crossword>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Crossword}]\index{Crossword@\probname{Crossword} problem}
Given an $n\times n$ grid, and a set of $2n$s-many strings, each
of length~$n$, decide if the
\citetext{words can be packed into the grid}{%
  The earliest known example is the Sator Square,\index{Stator Square} 
  five Latin words that pack into a grid.
  \begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=.75in]{complexity/pix/sator.jpg}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{tabular}{ccccc}
  S &A &T &O &R  \\
  A &R &E &P &O  \\
  T &E &N &E &T  \\
  O &P &E &R &A  \\
  R &O &T &A &S
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  % https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sator_Square
  % By M Disdero (Taken at Oppede, Luberon, France) [GFDL (http://www.gnu.org/copyleft/fdl.html) or CC BY-SA 3.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/3.0)], via Wikimedia Commons
  % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/71/Sator_Square_at_Opp%C3%A8de.jpg
  It appears in many places in the Roman Empire, often as graffiti.
  For instance, it
  was found in the ruins of Pompeii.
  Like many word game solutions it sacrifices comprehension for form but
  it is a perfectly grammatical sentence that translates as something like,
  ``The farmer Arepo works the wheel with effort.''
  % See https://www.academia.edu/5111444/_Magic_Squares_Alphabet_Jumbles_Riddles_and_more_The_culture_of_word-games_among_the_graffiti_of_Pompeii_in_The_Muse_at_Play._Riddles_and_Wordplay_in_Greek_and_Latin_Poetry_eds._J._Kwapisz_D._Petrain_and_M._Szymanski_Munich_De_Gruyter_2012_65-80
}.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Crossword>

\begin{example} % http://cgi.csc.liv.ac.uk/~ped/teachadmin/COMP202/annotated_np.html
Can we pack the words
\str{AGE}, \str{AGO}, \str{BEG}, \str{CAB}, \str{CAD}, and \str{DOG}
into a $3\times 3$ grid?
\begin{animatedfigure}{Instance of the Crossword
  problem}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=last]{2}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{00}{01}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\end{example}

%<*prob:FifteenGame>
\begin{problem}[\probname{$15$ Game}]\index{15 Game@\probname{$15$ Game} problem}
Given an $n\times n$ grid holding tiles numbered $1$, \ldots\,~$n-1$,
and a blank,
find the minimum number of moves that will put the tile numbers into
ascending order.
A move consists of switching a tile with an adjacent blank.
\end{problem}
%</prob:FifteenGame>

This game was
\citetext{popularized as a toy}{%
  It was invented by Noyes Palmer Chapman, a postmaster in Canastota, New York.
  As early as 1874 he showed friends a precursor puzzle.
  By December~1879
  copies of the improved puzzle were circulating in the northeast
  and students in the American School for the Deaf and other
  started manufacturing it.
  They become popular as the ``Gem Puzzle.''
  Noyes Chapman had applied for a patent in February, 1880. 
  By that time the game had became a craze in the US,
  somewhat like Rubik's Cube a century later.
  It was also popular in Canada and Europe.
  See~\autocite{wiki:FifteenPuzzle}}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics[width=0.25\textwidth]{complexity/pix/15game.png}
\end{center}

The final three problems may seem inextricably linked, but
as we understand them today, they seem to have different behavior. 
% In 1801, Gauss gave two reasons to solve them,
% ``The problem of distinguishing prime numbers from composite numbers
% and of resolving the latter into their prime factors is known to be one
% of the most important and useful in arithmetic.
% It has engaged the industry and wisdom of ancient and modern geometers
% to such an extent that it
% would be superfluous to discuss the problem at length \ldots{}
% Further, the dignity of the science itself
% seems to require that every possible means be explored for the solution of a
% problem so elegant and so celebrated.''

%<*prob:Divisors>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Divisor}]\index{Divisor@\probname{Divisor} problem}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, find a nontrivial divisor.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Divisors>

When the numbers are sufficiently large,
\citetext{we know of no efficient algorithm to find divisors}{% https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integer_factorization
  An effort in 2009 to factor a $768$-bit number ($232$-digits) used
  hundreds of machines and took two years.
  The researchers estimated that a $1024$-bit number would take about
  a thousand times as long.}.\footnote{%
  No efficient algorithm is known on a non-quantum computer.}
However, it must be said that at this time
we also have no proof that no efficient algorithm exists.\footnote{%
  There is no proof despite centuries of ingenious attacks on the problems
  by many of the brightest minds of the past, and of today.
  The presumed difficulty of this problem is at the heart of widely used
  algorithms in cryptography.}
Not all numbers of a given length are equally hard to factor.
The hardest numbers to factor, using the best currently known techniques,
are semiprimes, the product of two prime numbers.

%<*prob:PrimeFactorization>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Prime Factorization}]\index{Prime Factorization@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}  \label{prob:Factorization}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, produce its decomposition into a product of primes.
\end{problem}
%</prob:PrimeFactorization>

\citetext{Factoring seems, as far as we know today, to be hard}{%
  Finding factors has for many years been thought hard.
  For instance, a number is called a Mersenne prime
  if it is a prime number of the form $2^n-1$.
  They are named after M~Mersenne, a French friar and important figure in
  the early sharing of scientific results,
  who studied them in the early 1600's.
  He observed that
  if $n$ is prime then $2^n-1$ may be prime, for instance with $n=3$,
  $n=7$, $n=31$, and $n=127$.
  He suspected that others of that form were also prime, in particular
  $n=67$.

  On \ymd{1903}{10}{31}{} F~N Cole,
  then Secretary of the American Mathematical Society,
  made a presentation at a math meeting.
  When introduced, he went to the chalkboard and in complete silence
  computed $2^{67}-1=147\,573\,952\,589\,676\,412\,927$.
  He then moved to the other side of the board, wrote
  $193\,707\,721$ times $761\,838\,257\,287$, and worked through the
  calculation, finally finding equality.
  When he was done Cole returned to his seat, having not uttered a word
  in the hour-long presentation.
  His audience gave him a standing ovation.

  Cole later said that finding the factors had been a significant effort,
  taking ``three years of Sundays.''}.
What about if you only want to know whether a number is prime or composite,
and don't care about its factors?

%<*prob:Composite>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Composite}]\index{Composite@\probname{Composite} problem}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, determine if it has any nontrivial factors;
that is, decide if there is a number~$a$ that divides~$n$ and
such that $1<a<n$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Composite>

\margingraphic{0.25\textwidth}{complexity/pix/aks.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AKS_primality_test}{Nitin Saxena (b~1981), Neeraj Kayal (b~1979), Manindra Agrawal (b~1966)}}\index{Saxena, N!picture}\index{Kayal, N!picture}\index{Agrawal, M!picture} % http://www.horizons-2000.org/9.%20Active%20Projects/read/PRIMES%20is%20in%20P.htm; came from MAA focus?
For many years the consensus among experts was that \probname{Composite}
was probably quite hard.\footnote{%
  There are a number of probabilistic algorithms that are often
  used in practice that can test
  primality very quickly, with an extremely small chance of error.}
After all, for centuries many very smart people had worked on composites
and primes, and none of them had produced a fast test.
But in 2002 M~Agrawal, N~Kayal, and N~Saxena
proved that primality testing can be done in time polynomial 
in the number of digits of the number.
At this point refinments of their technique run in
$\bigOh(n^6)$.
This is known as the AKS primality test.\footnote{
  At the time they did most of the work, Kayal and Saxena were undergraduates.}\index{AKS primality test}

On thing this test does is dramatically illustrate the lesson
that although experts are expert and
their opinions have value, nonetheless they can be wrong.
People producing a result that gainsays established orthodoxy
has happened before and will no doubt happen again\Dash
one correct proof outweighs any number of opinions.

\begin{exparts*}

\item $52\,424$
\item $9600$

\item One is $2$ and another is~$4$.
\item One is $5$ and another is~$10$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Decide if each formula is satisfiable.
\begin{exparts}
\item $(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$
\item $(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This formula is satisfiable. 
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg Q$ &$\neg Q\wedge R$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item This formula is not satisfiable. 
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\to Q$ &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg P$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P$ &$\neg(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  &$(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:FiveColors}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
In \probname{Four Color} we require that the countries be
contiguous, that they not consist of separated regions.
Give a map that consists of separated regions that requires
five colors.
\item We also define adjacent to mean sharing a border that is an interval,
not just a point.
Give a map that would require five colors if we drop that requirement.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This part of a map shows five countries.
Obviously, country~$A$ must be a different color than any of the others.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems22.pdf}
\end{center}
Now add to that map a part where the large country is~$B$ and the other
four coutries have small circles,
one where the large country is~$C$, and one each for $D$ and~$E$.
In doing this we are allowing countries to consist of non-contiguous regions.
The result is five countries where each must be a different color than the
others.
\item Make a circle and split it into five wedges.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  % based on http://www.herinst.org/BusinessManagedDemocracy/introduction/interlocking.html
This shows interlocking corporate directorships.
The vertices are corporations and 
they are connected if they share a member of their Board of Directors
(the data is from 2004).
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems27.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Can you get, vertex-to-vertex, from AT\&T to Ford Motor?
\item Can you get from Haliburton to Ford Motor?
\item Can you get from Caterpillar to Ford Motor?
\item JP Morgan to Ford Motor?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item AT\&T connects to Citigroup, which connects to Ford Motor.
\item Haliburton connects to Citigroup, which connects to Ford Motor.
\item There is no connection between Caterpillar and Ford Motor.
\item Again, as shown, no connection.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
A popular game extends \probname{Vertex-to-vertex Path} 
by counting the separation.
Below is a portion of the movie connection graph, 
where vertices are actors and they are connected if they have ever been 
together in a movie.\footnote{%
  Information from \protect\url{https://oracleofbacon.org/}}
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems24.pdf}
\end{center}
Let someone's 
\definend{Bacon number} be the number of edges 
connecting them to Bacon, or infinity if they are not connected.
The game \citetext{\textit{Six Degrees of Kevin Bacon}}{%
  One night, three college friends,
  Brian Turtle, Mike Ginelli, and Craig Fass,
  were watching movies. 
  \textit{Footloose} was followed by \textit{Quicksilver}, 
  and between was a commercial for a third Kevin Bacon movie. 
  It seemed like Kevin Bacon was in everything! 
  This prompted the question of whether Bacon had ever worked with 
  De Niro? 
  The answer at that time was no, but De Niro was in 
  \textit{The Untouchables} with Kevin Costner, who was in 
  \textit{JFK} with Bacon. 
  The game was born.
  It became popular when they wrote to Jon Stewart about it and appeared
  on his show.
  (From \autocite{blog:Blanda}.)} 
asks:~is everyone connected to Kevin Bacon by at most six movies?
\begin{exparts}
\item What is Elvis's Bacon number?
\item John Kennedy's (no, it is not that John Kennedy)?
\item Bacon's?
\item How many movies separate me from Meryl Streep?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item 2
\item 2
\item 0
\item 4 (Really, this is not true.  I was only in the deleted scenes.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
This \probname{Knapsack} instance has no solution when the weight bound is 
$B=73$ and the value target is $T=140$.
\begin{center}\small 
\begin{tabular}{r|ccccc}
  \tabulartext{Item}
    &\tabulartext{a} &\tabulartext{b} &\tabulartext{c} &\tabulartext{d} &\tabulartext{e}  \\
  \cline{2-6}
  \rule{0pt}{9pt}  % scooch more room
  \tabulartext{Weight}  &$21$ &$33$ &$49$  &$42$  &$19$  \\
  \tabulartext{Value}   &$50$ &$48$ &$34$  &$44$  &$40$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Verify that by brute force, by checking every possible packing attempt.
(Probably the fastest way would be to write a program.)
\begin{answer}
Here are the packings, with their total weights and values.
\begin{center} \small
\begin{tabular}{ccccc|rr}   
   \tabulartext{a} 
     &\tabulartext{b}
     &\tabulartext{c}
     &\tabulartext{d}
     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{e}}
     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Weight}}
     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Value}}   \\ \hline
   N &N &N &N &N &$0$ &$0$ \\
   N &N &N &N &Y &$19$ &$40$ \\
   N &N &N &Y &N &$42$ &$44$ \\
   N &N &N &Y &Y &$61$ &$84$ \\
   N &N &Y &N &N &$49$ &$34$ \\
   N &N &Y &N &Y &$68$ &$74$ \\

\begin{exercise}
Find the shortest path in this graph
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems28.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item from $q_2$ to~$q_7$,
\item from $q_0$ to~$q_8$,
\item from $q_8$ to~$q_0$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
We can do these by eye.
\begin{exparts}
\item The shortest is $q_2$ to~$q_1$ to~$q_7$, which has length~$14$.
\item The shortest is $q_0$ to~$q_3$ to~$q_4$ to~$q_8$, of length~$23$.
\item There is no path between these two vertices.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Imagine a directed graph where the edges are prone to failure.
For instance, it may be a road network in a region prone to flooding.
For each edge we know the probability that the edge will fail.
Show how to use a solution to the Shortest Path problem to 
find the path between two vertices least likely to fail.
\begin{answer}
Let the probability of success be one minus the probability of failure.
Recall that logarithms turn multiplication into 
addition:~$\log(p_0\cdot p_1\cdots p_k)=log(p_0)+\cdots+\log(p_k)$.
Label each edge with the negative of the logarithm (any base) of the 
probability of success.
Minimizing the negative of the sum of the logarithms is maximizing
the positive sum.
That's the same as maximizing the product of the probabilities of 
success, which is the overall probability of success.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:CourseScheduling}
The \definend{\probname{Course Scheduling}}\index{Course Scheduling@\probname{Course Scheduling} problem}  
problem 
starts with a list of students and the 
classes they wish to take and then finds how many time slots are needed
to schedule the classes.
Of course, it assumes 
that if there is a student taking two classes then those two will not meet 
at the same time.
Here is an instance:~a
a school has classes in Astronomy, Biology, 
Computing, Drama, English, French, Geography, History, and~Italian.
After students sign up,
the graph below shows which classes have an overlap.
For instance
Astronomy and Biology share at least one student while Biology and Drama
do not.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs09.pdf}
  % \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs10.pdf}
\end{center}
What is the minimum number of class times that we must use?  
In coloring terms, we define that classes meeting at the same time
are the same color and we ask for the minimum number of colors needed so that
no two same-colored vertices share an edge.
\begin{answer}
No three-coloring suffices.
The three vertices $A$, $C$, and~$D$ form a triangle, so they must be different
colors.
Since $CDF$ is also a triangle, we conclude that $A$ and $F$ are the same
colors. 
So far we have:
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D}$.

Next consider triangle $DFI$. It's presence gives that $C$ and~$I$ are the same
colors.
So we have 
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D}$.
Then triangle $EFI$ leads to 
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D,E}$.

Finally, triangle $CFG$ gives that $D$ and~$G$ are the same color, giving
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D,E,G}$.
But the contradicts the presence of triangle $GEF$.

This is a four-coloring.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs10.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Some authors define \probname{Satisfiability}\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem} 
as:~given
a finite set of propositional logic statements,
find if there is a single input tuple $b_0,\ldots b_{j-1}$
(where each $b_i$ is either $T$ or $F$) that satisfies them all.
Show that this is equivalent to the definition given in \cref{prob:SAT}.
\begin{answer}
A finite set of propositional logic statements $\set{S_0,\ldots S_{k-1}}$
is satisfiable if and only if the single statement
$S_0\wedge S_1\ldots \wedge S_{k-1}$ is satisfiable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What shape is a $3$-clique?
A $2$-clique?
\begin{answer}
A $3$-clique is a triangle.
A $2$-clique is an edge.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Show that any subgraph of a clique is itself a clique.
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
How many edges does a $k$-clique have?
\begin{answer}
A $k$-clique has $k(k-1)/2$ edges, one for each combination of two vertices.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

Is there a $3$-clique in this graph?
A $4$-clique?
A $5$-clique?
\begin{center}

It has a $4$-clique, and thus necessarily a $3$-clique

but it has no $5$-clique, as you can check by exhaustion.
\end{answer}

\begin{exercise}
Recall that 
\probname{Vertex Cover}\index{Vertex Cover@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem} 
inputs a graph
$\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}$ and a number~$k\in\N$,
and asks if there is a subset~$S$ of at most $k$~vertices 
such that for each edge at least one endpoint is an element of~$S$.
The \definend{\probname{Independent Set}}\index{Independent Set@\probname{Independent Set} problem}
problem inputs a graph and a 
number~$\hat{k}\in\N$
and asks if there is a subset~$\hat{S}$ with at least $\hat{k}$~vertices 
such that for each edge at most one endpoint is in~$\hat{S}$.
The two are obviously related.
\begin{exparts}
\item
In this graph
find a vertex cover~$S$ with $k=2$ elements.
Find an independent set with $\hat{k}=4$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems29.pdf}
\end{center}
\item
In this graph
find a vertex cover with $k=3$ elements,
and an independent set with $\hat{k}=3$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems30.pdf}
\end{center} 
\item
In this graph
find a vertex cover~$S$ with $k=4$ elements.
Find an independent set~$\hat{S}$ with $\hat{k}=6$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity22.pdf}
\end{center}
\item Prove that $S$ is a vertex cover if and only if
its complement~$\hat{S}=\mathcal{V}-S$ is an independent set.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item A vertex cover with $k=2$ elements is $S=\set{q_1,q_3}$.
An independent set with $\hat{k}=4$ elements is 
$\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_4,q_5}$.
\item A vertex cover with $k=3$ elements is $S=\set{q_1,q_2,q_4d}$.
An independent set with $\hat{k}=3$ elements is 
$\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_3,q_5}$.
\item A vertex cover with $k=4$ elements is $S=\set{q_2,q_5,q_8,q_9}$.
An independent set with $\hat{k}=6$ elements is 
  $\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4,q_6,q_7}$.
\item For any edge, if it has at least one endpoint in
$S$ then it has at most one endpoint in the complement $\hat{S}=\mathcal{V}-S$.
Conversely, If an edge has at most one endpoint in~$\hat{S}$ 
then it has at least one endpoint in the complement~$S=\mathcal{V}-\hat{S}$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
