\index{problem miscellany|)}







% =======================================================
\section{Problems, algorithms, and programs} \label{sec:ProbsAlgosPrograms}
Now, with a good number of examples in hand,
we will reflect on a number of aspects of problems, and solutions.
We will discuss this on an intuitive level only\Dash indeed, 
some of these things have no widely accepted
precise definition.

%<*discussion:Problem>
A problem is a job, a task.\index{problem}
Somewhat more precisely, it is a 
\citetext{uniform family of tasks}{From \autocite{Jones}.}, 
typically unbounded in size. 
We are most focused on problems that can be solved with a mechanism,
although we continue to be interested to find 
that a problem we are studying
cannot be solved mechanically at all.
%</discussion:Problem>

For a sense of `family', 
contrast the general problem of \probname{Shortest Path}
with the instance of finding the shortest path between 
Los Angeles and New York. 
We are more likely to talk about the first than the second,
both because the second is a special case and because 
the first feels more natural.\footnote{There are interesting problems
  with only one task, such as
  computing the digits of $\pi$.}

%<*discussion:Algorithm>
An algorithm describes at a high level an effective way to solve the 
problem.\footnote{% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/31953
  \citetext{There is no widely-accepted formal definition of `algorithm'}{%
    This discussion derives from  \autocite{pseudonym}.}.
  Whatever it is, 
  it fits between `mathematical function' and `computer program'.
  For example, a `sort' function takes in a set of 
  items and returns the sorted sequence.
  This behavior could be implemented using different algorithms:~merge
  sort, heap sort, etc.
  In turn, each algorithm could be implemented by many programs,
  written in different languages and for different platforms.
  So the best handle that we have is informal\Dash an `algorithm' is
  an equivalence class of programs (i.e., Turing machines),
  where two programs are equivalent
  if they do essentially the same thing.\index{algorithm!definition}
  % Any two programs which implement the same algorithm must compute the same
  % function, but the converse is not true.
  % Similarly, there is an equivalence class between algorithms, where two
  % algorithms are equivalent if they compute the same mathematical function.  
  % The hard part in all this is trying to capture what we mean by
  % ``essentially the same thing.''
  % There are some things that we obviously should include.
  % For example, fix a program that does a merge sort on sequences of strings
  % and that uses \str{x} for a variable but not \str{y}.
  % The program that you get by simultaneously replacing all the
  % \str{x}'s with \str{y}'s is essentially the same.
  % And, there are some things that we may want not to include; an
  % operating system kernel is a single program but implements many
  % algorithms.
  % But in any event, giving a completely satisfactory
  % definition of `algorithm' is an open problem.
}\index{algorithm}
An algorithm is not an implementation,
although it should be described in a way that is 
detailed enough that implementing it is
routine for an experienced professional.

One subtle point about algorithms 
is that while they are abstractions, they are nonetheless based on
an underlying computing model.
An algorithm that is based on a Turing machine model
for adding one to an input 
would be very different than
an algorithm to do the same task on a model that is like
a desktop computer, such as the RAM model.\index{algorithm!reliance on model}
%</discussion:Algorithm>

\margingraphic*{0.20\textwidth}{languages/pix/sah.jpg}
An example of a very different computing model that an algorithm could target
is distributed computation.\index{distributed computation}\index{computation!distributed}\index{SETI"@home}
For example, the SETI@home project is a scientific experiment, 
% based at the University of California Berkeley, 
where volunteers install on their computer a program which 
downloads and analyzes blocks of data from a radio telescope.
This is massively parallel computation.\footnote{%
  It searches for extraterrestrial intelligence.
  It is Free and you can easily run it on your computer.
  \citetext{Visit \protect\url{http://setiathome.berkeley.edu/}}{%
    For more, see also \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_distributed_computing_projects}.}.}\index{computation!parallel}

%<*discussion:Program>
A program\index{program} is an implementation of an algorithm,
typically expressed in a formal computer language and 
designed to be executed on a specific computing platform.
%</discussion:Program>

To illustrate the differences between the problems, algorithms, and programs,
consider the problem \probname{Prime Factorization}.
One algorithm is to use brute force, that is, to
check every number less than the input.
We could implement that with a program written in Scheme.



\subsection{Statements and representations}
Strictly speaking,
a complete description of a problem must include the form of the
inputs and outputs.
For instance, if we state a problem 
as:~`\citetext{input two numbers and output their midpoint}{% 
  See \protect\url{https://hal.archives-ouvertes.fr/file/index/docid/576641/filename/computing-midpoint.pdf}.}'
then we have not fully specified what needs to be done.
The input might be 
strings representing decimal numbers or
might be in unary,
\citetext{where the number $n$ is represented with $n$-many \str{1}'s}{%
  An experienced programmer may have the reaction that unary is rediculous
  because it is wasteful\Dash
  if the input
  is one thousand then this representation would require the 
  machine to take a thousand steps just to read the input, 
  while in binary notation the input is $\str{1111101000}$, which 
  takes only ten steps to read.
  But unary is not completely useless;~we have found that it
  suited our purpose in the past, when we simply wanted to illustrate
  Turing machines with a minimum of overhead detail.}.
% Or we could specify something more exotic
% such as putting the sequence numbers as 
% powers of primes, so that~$\sequence{2,1,3}$ becomes 
% the single number $2^23^15^3=1500$ and then we represent that in a string of
% decimal digits.

The representation of the data both matters and doesn't.
One way that it
matters is that the form of the input and output can change the algorithm
that we think to apply, or its runtime behavior.
Suppose that we want to  decide whether an input number is
divisible by four.
For binary the algorithm is immediate:~a number is divisible by four 
if and only if in its binary representation 
\citetext{the final two bits are \str{00}}{%
  Decimal representation is not much harder since
  a decimal number is divisible by four if and only if the final two digits
  are in the set $\set{\str{00},\str{04}, \,\ldots\,\str{96}}$.}.
For unary we think to scan the \str{1}'s, keeping track
of the current remainder modulo four.
However, the representation doesn't matter in the sense that 
we could handle unary inputs by converting
them to binary and then applying the binary algorithm.
That is, in principle for Turing machines, 
with an algorithm for one represenation we can solve 
the problem for other represenatations by translating the inputs, 
applying the algorithm,
and translating to give the outputs.
We can worry that this might be inefficient but it at least gives 
an answer.

% \margingraphic{0.2\textwidth}{complexity/pix/norm.png}{\label{fig:xkcdnorm}\href{https://xkcd.com/2116/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
To provide some uniformity of representation,\index{representation, of a problem}
we will adopt the point of view
which we can call \definend{Lipton's Thesis}\index{Lipton's Thesis} that 
\citetext{everthing of interest can be represented, reasonably efficiently, by bitstrings}{%
  See
  \url{https://rjlipton.wordpress.com/2010/11/07/what-is-a-complexity-class/}.
  Of course, a wag may well say that
  if it cannot be represented by 
  bitstrings then it isn't of interest.
  But we mean something less tautological:~we mean that if we could 
  want to compute with it then it can be put in bitstrings.
  For example, we find that we can process speech, adjust colors, 
  or regulate pressure in a rocket fuel tank, all in bitstrings,
  despite what may at first encounter seem to be the inheriently 
  analog nature of thiese things.}.\footnote{%
  Here, `reasonable' means that it is not so inefficient as to greatly
  change the big-$\bigOh$ behavior.}
That is, compared to the description of \probname{Shortest Path}
given as \cref{prob:ShortestPath}, we will understand that a more 
completely specification is:~``given
a reasonable bit string representation of a weighted graph~$\bar{\mathcal{G}}$ 
and two vertices, $\bar{v_0}$ and~$\bar{v_1}$,
return a reasonable bit string representation of the shortest path between 
them.''
This includes all of the mathematically-flavored problems 
described earlier.
But it also includes things that seem less natural, such as that we 
can with bit strings faithfully represent 
\citetext{Beethoven's 9th Symphony}{The official story is that
CD's are $72$ minutes long so that they can hold this piece.}
or an exquisite Old Master.
\begin{fig}{\textit{Basket of Fruit} by Caravaggio (1571--1610)} % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e5/Basket_of_Fruit-Caravaggio_%28c.1595%29.jpg
  \includegraphics[width=0.8\textwidth]{complexity/pix/caravaggio.jpg}
\end{fig}
\noindent 
Bit strings are convenient, straightforward, and familiar.
But the assertion that they always suffice to
give a representation that is efficient enough is 
in some ways like Church's Thesis:~we
cannot prove it but experience\Dash particularly very recent 
experience\Dash argues that it is so.
% This mindset has the advantage of uniformity:~we can stick to bit strings
% and not worry that we are missing out. 

However, having observed the need for representations  
and that we will use bit strings,
from now on we will usually not mention it.
We will usually leave
\citetext{detailed specifications for the programmer}{%
  \autocite{article:MarsOrbiter}},
because they will typically not affect the outcome of our analysis much,
as long as they are reasonable.



\subsection{Types of problems}
There are patterns in which problems we see in the Theory of Computation.
One problem type that we have already seen
is a 
\definend{function problem}.\index{function problem}\index{problem!function}
This asks that the algorithm have a single output for each input. 
Two examples are \probname{Prime Factorization}
and the problem of finding the greatest common divisor of two
natural numbers.

A second type 
is the 
\definend{optimization problem},\index{optimization problem}\index{problem!optimization}
which looks for the 
best solution according to some metric.
\probname{Shortest Path} is of this type.
So is \probname{Minimal Spanning Tree}.

Another type is a 
\definend{search problem}.\index{search problem}\index{problem!search}
Here, while there may be many
solutions in the search space, we stop when we have found any one.
An example is the problem, 
``Given a weighted graph, and two vertices, and a bound $B\in\N$,
find a path between the vertices that costs less than the bound.'' 
Another example is that of finding a $B$-coloring for a graph; there
may be many colorings but we just want one.
Two more are the Knapsack problem and the Subset Sum problem.

A  problem with a `Yes' or `No' answer is a 
\definend{decision problem}.\index{decision problem}\index{problem!decision}
The first problem we saw, the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem},\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}}
is one of these.\footnote{%
  Recall that the word is German for ``decision problem''
  and that it asks for an algorithm to decide, given a mathematical statement,
  whether the statement is true or false.}
Another is the problem of deciding whether a given natural number is prime,
and another is \probname{Clique}.
We have also seen decision problems 
in conjunction with the \HP{}, such as the problem of
determining, given an index~$e$, whether $\TMfcn_e$ will 
output a seven for any input.

Although a decision problem calls for producing a function of a kind,
a Boolean function,
they are common enough to be a separate category.

Often a decision problem is expressed as a
\definend{language decison problem},\index{language decision problem}\index{problem!language decision}\index{language!decision problem}
or \definend{language recognition problem},\index{language recognition problem}\index{problem!language recognition}
where we are given some language and asked for an algorithm to
decide if the input is a member of that language.
We did lots of these in the Automata chapter, such as producing a machine that 
decides if an input string is a member of  
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ contains at least two \str{b}'s}}$,
or proving that no Finite State machine can determine membership
in $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.

One reason for our interest in problems of language membership
arises from practice; for instance, a language complier must recognize whether
a given source file is a member of the language.
Another is that Finite State machines can only do one thing, 
decide languages, and so to compare these with other machines 
we must do so via which languages they can decide.
Still another reason is that in many contexts stating a problem in this
way is natural, as we saw with the \HP.

From this point on, 
by `problem' we will usually mean a decision problem for a language.
(We will be sloppy about the distinction between the
decision problem for a language and the language itself; for instance, we
often use the letter $\prob$ for a problem.)
Often, that will mean recasting problems to put them into language decision 
terms.

\begin{example}
Recall \probname{Satisfiability}.\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem!as a language recognition problem} 
As stated, it is a yes-or-no decision problem.
We can recast it as the problem of determining membership in this language
$
  \SAT=
  \set{F\suchthat
    \text{$F$ is a satisfiable propositional logic statement}}
$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Similarly, we can recast the problem of determining whether a given number 
is prime to the problem of determining whether a given representation of~$n$
is a member of 
$\set{r\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$r$ represents a prime number}}$.
\end{example}

Those recastings are straightforward.
For optimization problems  
there is a standard technique 
to reword the optimization as a sequence of language decision problems.

\begin{example}
Recall that \probname{Chromatic Number} inputs a graph 
and returns a minimal 
number~$B\in\N$ such that the graph is $B$-colorable.
Recast it by:~for each $B\in\N$ define the language
$\lang_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat \text{$\mathcal{G}$ has a $B$-coloring}}$.
Then, given a graph, if we could solve the language decision problem for 
each~$B$ then we can find the minimal chromatic number by 
testing whether it has a $B=1$-coloring, 
then testing whether it has a $B=2$-coloring, etc., 
until we find the smallest such~$B$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
\probname{Travelling Salesman}, as stated in the prior section, 
is an optimization problem.
Recast it as a language decision problem in a way like the prior example:~set 
a bound~$B\in\N$ and consider this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \TS_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat
    \text{$\mathcal{G}$ is a weighted graph with a circuit~$\mathcal{C}$ where 
          $\lh{\mathcal{C}}\leq B$}}
\end{equation*}
Now, given a weighted graph, if we could solve the language decision
problem for each~$B$ then 
to find the least integer for which there is a circuit
we can ask if that graph is a member of~$\mathcal{TS}_1$, if it is a member 
of~$\mathcal{TS}_2$, etc.\sentencespace
When we find a $B$ then that gives the length of the shortest circuit.
\end{example}

The recasting must
\citetext{capture the essential difficulty in the problem}{%
  % from https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/79729
  Not every computational problem is easy to express as a decision problem 
  for a language in a way that is natural.
  One example is the problem which, given an input string will
  return the string with the characters sorted into ascending order.
  We could recast it as the language of sorted strings
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ is sorted}}$. 
  But recognizing a sorted input $\sigma$ 
  does not seem to require that we find a good way 
  to sort an unsorted input.
  Better is to consider the language of pairs
  $\sequence{\sigma,p}$ where $p$ is a permutation of the numbers
  $0,\ldots \lh{\sigma}-1$ that brings the string into ascending order.
  Here also the recast formulation 
  seems to not capture the sorting problem in that recognizing a
  correct permutation seems different than generating one from scratch.
  (Besides the informal feeling that they miss the mark, 
  precise evidence that these do not get at the nut of the search problem is
  that  we can decide both languages in time that is
  $\bigOh(n)$ where $n$ is the input size,
  whereas sorting takes $\bigOh(n\lg n)$ time.)}.
% More precisely, we want that a fast way to solve the language 
% decision problem translates to a fast way to solve the 
% problem before it was recast.
Note that the above optimization problems 
will be solvable in polytime
if and only if the associated parametrized problems are solvable 
in polytime\Dash searching through $B=0$, $B=1$, etc, only adds a
linear factor. 

% Is limiting ourselves to language decision problems too restricitve?
% Consider the problem of finding integer roots for a polynomial.
% The function problem that starts with a polynomial and returns a
% root, if any, intuitively seems slower than the problem of verifying
% that a pair $\sequence{p,r}$ is in the language
% $\set{\sequence{p,r}\suchthat p(r)=0}$.
% However, we will consider classes
% of problems that are closed under the difference, in that if  
% a fast way to solve the decision problem is in the class then
% we can translate it to 
% a fast way to solve the function problem, and vice versa.

% In summary, we are thinking of problems as language recognition problems,
% and as all the work happening with bit strings.
% So we can rework the informal idea of `problem' in this way.

% %<*def:Problem>
% \begin{definition}
% A \definend{problem} is a language decision problem.
% \end{definition}
% %</def:Problem>


% \begin{fig}[fig:BeanOfProblems]{The bean encloses all problems.
%   Since a problem is a decision problem for a language, this is basically
%   the set of languages.
%   We can imagine that the ones with a fast algorithm are at the bottom, and 
%   then we move to toward problems whose best algorihtm is slow at the top.}
%   \setlength{\unitlength}{1cm}
%   \begin{picture}(0,0)
%     \put(0,0.8){\makebox[0cm][r]{\small The set $\kleenestar{\B}$:}}
%   \end{picture}
%   \hspace{-1.0em}  
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
%   \hspace{0.6em}
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp03.pdf}}
% \end{fig}

\begin{remark}
Because we are isolating language decision problems,
some authors adopt a modification of the definition of a Turing machine,
to have it come with a subset of accepting states.
Such a machine solves a problem
if it halts on all inputs~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$,
and when it halts it is in an accepting state exactly if $\sigma$ is in
the language.
\end{remark}

\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What is the difference\Dash speaking informally, since some of these 
do not have formal definitions\Dash between an algorithm and:
\begin{exparts*}
\item a heuristic,
\item pseudocode,
\item a Turing machine
\item a flowchart, and
\item a process?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item A heuristic is a method that, while it may be used in practice, 
is not guaranteed to be optimal, perfect, logical, or even rational.
An algorithm for a problem is guaranteed to solve the problem.
\item Pseudocode is more like a program than an algorithm, in that
it is tied to a particular kind of language.
For example, a person can give pseudocode that woule apply to any
Algol-like language but ano a Lisp-like one.
\item A Turing machine is more like a program than an algorithm, in that
it is an implementation on a specific hardware platform of an algorithm.
\item A flowchart is a graphical representation of an algorithm.
It might suppress more details than a writen representation does.
\item A process, such as an operating system shell or a web server, might 
be designed to run forever (or it might not).
An algorithm is guaranteed to halt.
(Another common use of `process' is that it is an instance of a program that has
been loaded into the machine for execution.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false:~any two programs that implement the same algorithm 
must compute the same function.
Is the converse true or false?
\begin{answer}
  It is true that two programs that implement the same algorithm 
  must compute the same function.
  It is false that two programs computing the same function must implement
  the same algorithm.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Solve the decision problem for
\begin{exparts*}
\item the empty language, and
\item the language~$\B$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The algorithm to solve this language decision problem is to, for all
input, return~$0$.
\item The algorithm here is to always return~$1$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Name something important that cannot be represented in binary.
\begin{answer}
The smell of a baby's head.
Baby's heads have a great smell.
But we don't compute it.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Identify each as a function problem, a decision problem, a search
problem, or an optimization problem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The problem \definend{\probname{Connectedness}}, which inputs a graph
and decides whether for any two vertices there is a path between them.
\item
The problem that inputs two natural numbers and returns their least
common multiple.
\item The \probname{Graph Isomorphism} problem 
that inputs two graphs and determines
whether they are isomorphic.
\item The problem that takes in a propositional logic statement and 
returns an assignment of truth values to its inputs that makes
the statement true (if there is such an assignment).
\item
The \definend{\probname{Nearest Neighbor}} problem 
that inputs a weighted graph and a vertex
returns the vertex nearest the given one (that does not equal the
given one).
\item The \definend{\probname{Discrete Logarithm}} problem:~given 
a prime number~$p$ and two
numbers $a,b\in\N$, determine if there is a power~$k\in\N$ so that
$a^k\equiv b \pmod p$.
\item The problem that inputs a bitstring and returns the number that it
represents in decimal.
\item The problem that takes a string and decides if there is a person in
the address book with that name.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{exparts}
\item This is a yes-or-no question.  
  So it is a decision problem.
\item Function problem.
\item Decision problem
\item Search problem
\item Optimization problem
\item Decision problem
\item Function problem
\item Decision problem 
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Express each decision problem as a one of accepting members of a 
language.   
\begin{exparts}
\item Decide whether a number is a perfect square.
\item Decide whether any triple $\sequence{x,y,z}\in\N^3$ is a 
   Pythagorean triple, whether $x^2+y^2=z^2$.
\item Decide whether a graph has an even number of edges.
\item Decide whether a path in a graph has any repeated vertices.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:RecastAsDecision}
Recast each to a language decision problem. 
\begin{exparts*}
\item
\probname{Euler Circuit}, \cref{prob:EulerCircuit}.
\item
\probname{Shortest Path}, \cref{prob:ShortestPath}.
\item
\probname{Graph Colorability}, \cref{prob:GraphColorability}.
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show how to use an algorithm that solves \probname{Shortest Path} to solve 
\probname{Vertex-to-vertex Path}.
How to use it on graphs that are not weighted?
\begin{answer}
Imagine that we have a routine \texttt{shortest\_path} that takes in a 
weighted graph and two vertices, and returns the shortest path between them.
That also solves the decision problem of whether there is any path at all.

If the graph is not weighted, convert it to a weighted graph by 
taking each edge to be of weight~$1$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Express each optimization problem as a language decision problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given a \probname{$15$ Game} board, 
find the least number of slides that will
solve it.
\item Given a Rubik's cube configuration, find the least number of moves
to solve it.
\item Given a list of jobs that must be accomplished to assemble a car,
along with how long each job takes and which jobs must be done before
other jobs, find the shortest time to finish the entire car.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Give a function problem version of \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}.
Also give an optimization version.
\begin{answer}
A function problem version would be to input a graph and
output a circuit.
An optimization problem would return a circuit that is best by some
criterion; for instance could, in a weighted graph, return the circuit
of least weight (this is \probname{Travelling Salesman}).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let
$
\lang_{F}=\set{\sequence{n,B}\in\N^2\suchthat
  \text{there is an $m\in\set{1,\ldots\,B}$ that divides $n$}}
$
and consider its language decision problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that $\sequence{d,B}\in\lang_{F}$ if and only if $B$ is greater
than or equal to the least prime factor of $d$.
\item
Conclude that you can use a solution to the language recognition problem
to solve the search problem of, given a number, returning a
prime factor of that number.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that with an algorithm that solves \probname{Subset Sum},
\cref{prob:SubsetSumDecision}, we can quickly solve the associated function
problem of finding the subset.
\begin{answer}
By taking the numbers in the set $S=\set{s_0,\ldots\,s_{n-1}}$ one at a 
time:~$S_0=\set{s_0}$, then $S_1=\set{s_0,s_1}$, \ldots\,,
you can figure out from the decision problem solver
what is the largest number~$s_j$ in the sum.
Subtract that from the goal and then iterate using $S-\set{s_j}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give and example where you can solve the decision problem instanteously
but you cannnot do so for the search problem.
\begin{answer}
Ever list of nubmers has a maximum, but finding it means at least looking
through the list.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Convert each function problem to an equivalent decision problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem that inputs two natural numbers and returns their
product.
\item The problem of \probname{Nearest Neighbor},
that inputs a weighted graph and a vertex and
returns the vertex nearest the given one but not equal to it. 
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Sketch an algorithm for each language recognition problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat\text{$\sigma$ is the decimal representation of a multiple of $100$}}$
\item
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\str{1}}}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ has more \str{1}'s than \str{0}'s}}$
\item $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma}$
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Convert each optimization problem into a family of decision problems.
\begin{exparts*}
\item \probname{Travelling Salesman} 
\item \probname{Shortest Path}
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language decision problem, give an algorithm that runs in~$\bigOh(1)$.
\begin{exparts}
\item The language of minimal-length binary representations of numbers 
that are nonzero.
\item The binary representations of numbers that exceed~$1000$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item See if the input string starts with a~\str{1} bit.
\item See if the input string has length less than or equal to ten.
If the length equals ten then there are some cases to do but that 
doesn't change that the algorithm is $\bigOh(1)$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show how to use an algorithm that solves \probname{Vertex-to-vertex Path}
to solve the problem \probname{Graph Connectedness}, which inputs a graph
and decides whether that graph is connected, so that
for any two vertices there is a path between them.
\begin{answer}
Check all pairs of vertices.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}






% =======================================
\section{$\P$}\index{P@$\P$|(}
Recall that 
a class\index{class}\index{complexity class} 
is a collection of languages.
%<*def:ComplexityClass>
\begin{definition}
A \definend{complexity class}\index{complexity class} 
is a collection of languages that can be
computed within some resource bound.
\end{definition}
%</def:ComplexityClass>

\begin{example}
The regular languages form a complexity class.
Remember that a language~$\lang$ is regular if there is a deterministic Finite
State machine that accepts it.
The work done by such a machine is a kind of computation\Dash basically,
A Finite State machine is a Turing machine that does not write\Dash and
when presented with a candidate string~$\sigma$ the number of steps that
the machine takes is linear, $\lh{\sigma}$. 
\end{example}

\begin{example}
We have already studied the class~\citetext{$\compclass{RE}$}{%
  Recall that `recursively enumerable' is an old-fashioned term
  for `computably ennumerable'.}\index{RE@$\compclass{RE}$, computably enumerable sets}\index{computably enumerable set!collection of, RE@collection of, $\compclass{RE}$} 
of languages~$\lang$ that are 
computably enumerable, that is, 
for which there is a computable function~$\TMfcn_e$ so that
the set $\set{k=f(i)\suchthat \text{$k=\TMfcn_e(i)$ for some input $i\in\N$}}$
equals the set~$\lang$.
Here the resource bound is that there is no finite time bound. 
\end{example}

\begin{example}
One complexity class is 
the collection of languages for which there is a Turing machine
that runs in time $\bigOh(n^{10})$. 
Restated, a language~$\lang$ is in that class if there is a Turing machine~$\TM$
that runs in time~$\map{t}{\N}{\N}$ where $t\in\bigOh(n^{10})$,
such that the associated function $\map{\TMfcn_{\TM}}{\N}{\B}$ 
has $\TMfcn_{\TM}(n)=1$ if $n\in\lang$ and
$\TMfcn_{\TM}(n)=0$ otherwise. 
\end{example}

Since a language is a set,
we are studying the characteristic functions of sets, and 
grouping them together based on how hard they are to 
compute.\footnote{%
  There are
  \citetext{many ways to do this}{%
    At this writing there are 535 studied ways but the number 
   changes frequently;
   see \protect\url{https://complexityzoo.uwaterloo.ca/Complexity_Zoo}.}.
  It may involve Turing machines or other machine types, such as 
  nondeterministic Turing machines 
  or Turing machines with access to an oracle for numbers that
  are truly random.
  It may also involve grouping problems by how long they take,
  or by their use of computational resources other than
  time, such as space.}
  % The most satisfactory definition for a grouping proves to be the one that
  % leaves the most freedom to consider whatever classes prove to be
  % of interest, which is why the definition of `complexity class' is 
  % so general; is it any collection of problems at all.}
Recall that in complexity theory we do not distinguish  
between problems\Dash that is, decision problems for a language\Dash and
the language itself.\index{language decision problem}
So we will often say that a complexity class is a collection of problems, 
or that problem is a member of a class.



\subsection{Definition} 
The complexity class that we introduce now  
is the most important one.
It is the collection of language decision problems 
that we take to be tractable under Cobham's Thesis. 

%<*def:P>
\begin{definition}
A problem is a member of the class 
\definend{$\P$}\index{P@$\P$}\index{polytime} 
if there is an algorithm for it that runs in
polynomial time on a deterministic Turing machine.
\end{definition}
%</def:P>

% This shows the class~$\P$ as a subset of the collection of all languages.
\begin{fig}[]{The bean encloses all
decision problems, $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$.
Shaded is the class~$\P$.
The shading is in layers because
$\P$ contains problems such that there is a solution algorithm  
that is $\bigOh(1)$,
problems for which there is an algorithm that is $\bigOh(n)$,
ones with a $\bigOh(n^2)$ algorithm, etc.}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp02.pdf}}
\end{fig}

One problem that is a member of~$\P$ is that of deciding the
language of perfect squares
$\lang_0=\set{n\in\N\suchthat \text{there is $k\in\N$ with $k^2=n$}}$
since given a natural number, no matter how large,
we can quickly decide if it is a square by 
at worst squaring all smaller numbers.
Here are some other languages whose decision problems are in~$\P$:\begin{parenum}
\item deciding the language of correct sums
$\lang_1=\set{\sequence{a,b,c}\in\N^3\suchthat a+b=c}$,
\item the language of sorted strings
$\lang_2=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\str{a},\ldots \str{z}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is in alphabetical order}}$,
\item the language of primes
$\lang_3=\set{k\suchthat\text{$k$ is decimal representation of a prime}}$,
\item For a regular expression~$R$, the language of strings satisfying
 that expression
$\lang_4=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
  \suchthat\text{$\sigma$ satisfies $R$}}$,
\item the language of pairs of connected nodes
$\lang_5=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}\suchthat\text{graph $\mathcal{G}$ has a path $v\hat{v}$}}$
(this is the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem).  
\end{parenum}

\begin{remark}
Again, note that 
the members of $\P$ are problems, essentially languages,
for which there exists a decision algorithm that is sufficiently fast.
Thus, it is wrong to say that an algorithm is in~$\P$.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Effect of the model of computation}
A problem is in~$\P$ if it has an algorithm that is polytime.
But algorithms are based on an underlying computing model.
Is membership in $\P$ dependent on the model that we use for computation?

In particular, experience with the Turing machine model 
gives a person the sense that they involve a lot of tape moving.
So a person may expect that they are slow.
However,
close analysis with a wide range of alternative computational models
proposed over the years shows that while
Turing machines algorithms 
are often slower than related algorithms for other natural models,\footnote{%
  We can take a model to be `natural' if it
  was not invented in order to be a counterexample to this.}
it is only by a factor of between $n^2$ and $n^4$.
That is, we have a problem for which there is a
$\bigOh(n)$ algorithm on
another model then we might find that on a Turing machine
model it may be $\bigOh(n^3)$, or $\bigOh(n^4)$, or~$\bigOh(n^5)$.
So it is still in~$\P$.

A variation of Church's thesis, the 
\definend{Extended Church's Thesis},\index{Extended Church's Thesis}\index{Church's Thesis!Extended} 
posits that not only are
all reasonable models of mechanical computation of equal power,
but in addition that they are of equivalent speed in that we can simulate any
reasonable model of computation\footnote{%
  One definition of `reasonable' is ``in principle physically realizable''
  \autocite{BernsteinVazirani}.} in polytime on a
probabilistic Turing machine.\footnote{%
  A Turing machine with a random oracle.}
This thesis does not enjoy anything like
the support of the original Church's Thesis.
But under the extended thesis, 
a problem that falls in the class~$\P$ using Turing machines
also falls in that class using other natural models.

\begin{remark}
Breaking news!
There is a challenge to the extended thesis. 
For some time we have known of several problems, 
including the familiar \probname{Prime Factorization},
that under the Quantum Computing\index{Quantum Computing} 
model have algorithms with polytime solutions
but for which we do not know of any polytime solution in a
non-quantum model.

What's more, quite recently engineers at Google Labs claimed to have
achieved 
\definend{Quantum Supremacy},\index{Quantum Supremacy} 
to have solved a problem
using an algorithm that runs on the Quantum Computing model that 
is not solvable on a machine on a Turing machine or RAM model in
less than centuries.
See \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-ZNEzzDcllU}{this YouTube video from Google} 
and 
\href{https://www.scottaaronson.com/blog/?p=4317}{S~Aaronson's Quantum Supremacy FAQ}.

But there are reservations.
For one, the claim is the subject of controversy;~see
\href{https://www.ibm.com/blogs/research/2019/10/on-quantum-supremacy/}{this posting from IBM Research} and
\href{https://gilkalai.wordpress.com/2019/11/13/gils-collegial-quantum-supremacy-skepticism-faq/}{A Quantum Supremecy Skepticism FAQ from researcher G~Kalai}.
% \url{https://gilkalai.wordpress.com/2019/09/23/quantum-computers-amazing-progress-google-ibm-and-extraordinary-but-probably-false-supremacy-claims-google/}
For another, this is not general purpose computing;~the problem solved
is exotic.
In addition, whether quantum computers will ever be practical physical devices 
used for everyday problems is not 
at this moment perfectly clear, 
although scientists and engineers are making
great progress developing them.
For the purposes of this book we put this event aside, as these devices
are not now available, but we will
be watching these events with great interest.
\end{remark}




  

 



\subsection{Naturalness}
We will give the class~$\P$ a lot of attention.
There are several reasons why it is natural to
study.

The first was described in the prior subsection.
Over the past century researchers have devised many
models of computation.
All of them compute the same set of functions as Turing machines\Dash
otherwise Church's Thesis would be invalidated\Dash but they may do so at
different speeds.
However, all these models\footnote{%
  All of the non-quantum natural models, anyway.} 
run within polytime of each other.
So $P$ is invariant under the choice of
computing model:~if a problem is in $P$ based on any familiar model
including Turing machines, RAM machines, and Racket programs under Linux, then
it is in~$P$ for all of them.
The differences between models washes out in the general polynomial sloppiness.
(Standard practice defines~$\P$ via Turing machines.)

Another reason that $P$ is a natural class of problems to study
is that it has a number of appealing closure properties.
%<*discussion:PClosure>
Fix a total function $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ and consider
the set
$\prob_f=\set{\sequence{\bar{n},\bar{f(n)}}\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat n\in\N}$,
where the bars mean that we represent the numbers in binary.
This set essentially turns the problem of computing~$f$ into 
the decision problem of accepting the language~$\prob_f$.
With that, $\P$ is closed under function addition,
scalar multiplication by an integer,
subtraction, multiplication, and composition.
It is also closed under concatenation,
so if $\lang\in\P$ and $\hat{\lang}\in\P$ then the concatenation
language $\lang\concat\hat{\lang}$ is also an element of~$\P$,
and the same holds for Kleene star.
%</discussion:PClosure>

But the main reason we are interested in $\P$ is Cobham's Thesis, that
a reasonable formalization of the intuitive idea that a 
problem is tractable is that it is in~$\P$. 
Insofar as theory should be a guide for practice, this is a compelling
reason.


\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or False: every problem that involves determining membership in a
finite language is in $\P$.
\begin{answer}
True.
Such problems have algorithms that are $\bigOh(1)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
This sentence is mistaken; describe what is wrong with it: ``I've got a problem
whose algorithm is in $\P$.''
\begin{answer}
Algorithms are not in $\P$, rather, problems are in $\P$ if from among all
the algorithms that solve them at least one algorithm is $\bigOh{n^c}$ for
some constant~$c$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What is the difference between a complexity class and an order of growth?
\begin{answer}
An order of growth is a set of functions, subject to some conditions.
A complexity class is a set of problems.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Is the \HP{} in $\P$?
\begin{answer}
The \HP{} is not decidable, so it is not in $\P$
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that any Regular language is in~$\P$.
\begin{exercise}
For any regular language, it is accepted by some Finite State machine.
A Finite State machine is a kind of Turing machine.
Each Finite State machine runs in linear time, since $t(\sigma)=n$ where
$\lh{\sigma}=n$.
Linear is polynomial, so each Regular language can be accepted in polytime.
\end{exercise}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Prove that the problem of deciding if two natural numbers is relatively
prime is in $\P$.
\begin{answer}
Give two natural numbers, 
use Euclid's algorithm to find if their greates common divisor is~$1$.
This is a polynomial time algorithm.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Prove that each problem is in $\P$ by citing the runtime of a particular
algorithm.
\begin{exparts}
\item Deciding the language of correct sums,
$\set{\sequence{a,b,c}\in\N^3\suchthat a+b=c}$.
\item Deciding the language
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\str{a},\ldots \str{z}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is in alphabetical order}}$.
\item Deciding
$\set{\sequence{A,B,C}\suchthat\text{the matrices are such that $AB=C$}}$.
\item Deciding the language of primes,
$\set{\str{1}^k\suchthat\text{$k$ is prime}}$.
\item Reachable nodes:
$\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1}\suchthat\text{the graph $\mathcal{G}$ has a path from $v_0$ to $v_1$}}$.  
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Find which of these is currently known to be in~$\P$
and which is not.
\hint~you may need to look up what is the fastest known algorithm.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
\probname{Shortest Path}
\item
\probname{Knapsack}
\item
\probname{Euler Path}
\item
\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item By Dijkstra's algorithm, this can be done in polynomial time.
  So the problem is in~$\P$.
\item \probname{Knapsack} is believed to be quite hard.
  No one knows of a polynomial algorithm.
\item \probname{Euler Path} is easy; we just need to check whether each
  node has even degree.
  There are algorithms that are linear.
\item No one knows of a polynomial algorithm for 
  \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}.  
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The problem of \probname{Graph Connectedness} is:~given a graph, 
decide if there is a path from any vertex to any other.
Sketch a proof that this problem is in~$\P$
\begin{answer}
Pick a vertex and mark it.
For the set of vertices connected to that one, mark them.
At each step thereafter, mark all vertices connected to any in the set
of already-marked vertices.

This algorithm is polynomial in the size of the graph.
Specifically, it will take $\bigOh(|\mathcal{V}|^2\cdot|\mathcal{E}|)$.
But since every edge consists of two vertices,
$|\mathcal{E}|\leq|\mathcal{V}|^2$.
Thus this algorithm is $\bigOh(|\mathcal{V}|^4)$
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that if $\mathcal{P}_1\in\P$ and if $\mathcal{P}_0\leq_p\mathcal{P}_1$
then $\mathcal{P}_0\in\P$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Sketch a proof that each problem is in~$\P$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
The $\prob[\tau]^3$ problem:~given a bitstring~$\sigma$, 
decide if it has the form
$\sigma=\tau\concat\tau\concat\tau$.
\item
The problem of deciding which Turing machines halt within ten steps.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Prove that $\P$ is closed under complement.
\begin{answer}
Assume that $\prob\in\P$.
Then there is a deterministic Turing machine~$\TM$ that,
given $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$, accepts it if and only if $\sigma\in\prob$,
and does so in polynomial time.
Then the Turing machine~$\hat{\TM}$ that is the same as~$\TM$ except that the
accepting and non-accepting states are swapped will accept
$\setcomp{\prob}$, also in polynomial time.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that $\P$ is closed under concatenation
and Kleene star.
\end{exercise}


% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that $\P$ is closed under composition: if $f_0,f_1$ are polynomial time
% functions and their composition is defined then $\composed{f_1}{f_0}$ is
% also polytime.
% \begin{answer}
% If $f_0$ is $\bigOh(n^{k_0})$ and $f_1$ is $\bitOh(n^{k_1})$ then the composition
% is $\bigOh(n^{k_1k_0})$.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Sketch a proof that every regular language is a member of $\P$.
% \begin{answer}
% Let $\lang$ be regular.
% Then there is a deterministic Finite State machine that accepts~$\lang$.

% We can create a program that simulates this machine.
% By Church's Thesis we can do it on a Turing machine.
% The number of steps that the simulation takes is linear in the length of the
% input, since the head is read-only.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} 
Consider the problem of 
\probname{Triangle}:~given\index{Triangle@\probname{Triangle} problem} 
an undirected graph, 
decide if it has a $3$-clique, three vertices that are mutually connected.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Why is this not \probname{Clique}?
\item
Sketch a proof that this problem is in~$\P$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
Because the size of the clique is fixed.
\item
Given $\mathcal{G}$, enumerate all triples
$\sequence{v_0,v_1,v_2}\in\mathcal{V}$
and check whether all three of $v_0v_1, v_0v_2, and v_1v_2$
are members of~$\mathcal{E}$.

This is a polynomial algorithm.
Specifically, it is $\bigOh(|\mathcal{V}|^3|\mathcal{E}|)$.
Because $|\mathcal{E}\leq |\mathcal{V}|^2|$, the algorithm is
$\bigOh(|\mathcal{V}|^5)$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
As in the section body, where $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ is a computable function
consider
the decision problem~$\prob_f$ for the language
$\set{\sequence{\bar{n},\bar{f(n)}}\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat n\in\N}$,
where the bar means the numbers are represented in binary.
In this way we can consider a function to be a member of~$\P$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Prove that the class~$\P$ is closed under function addition
\item
Prove that $P$ is closed under multiplication by a scalar~$r\in\R$.
\item
Prove it is closed under function subtraction.
\item
Prove $\P$ is closed under function multiplication.
\item
Prove it is closed under function composition.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the class of languages~$\P$ is closed under each operation.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Reversal
\item Union
\item Concatenation
\item Kleene star
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
We will show that making polynomially many calls to a subroutine that
is polynomial may take exponential time.
Consider a routine that may call a subroutine~\str{S}.
This subroutine is perfectly reasonable:~\str{S} runs in time linear 
in the input length, and given input of length~$n$ it returns 
output of length~$2n$. 
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that if the routine calls \str{S} once then
it costs a time that is polynomial in the length of the input.
\item Show that if the routine calls \str{S} twice, 
as $\str{S}(\str{S}(x))$, then
it costs a time that is polynomial in the length of the the input.
\item Ditto for a call that is nested $k$~deep for fixed~$k\in\N$.
\item In contrast, for variable~$k$ show that the call
\begin{equation*}
  \underbrace{\str{S}(\str{S}(\cdots\str{S}(x)\cdots))}_{\text{$k$ times}}
\end{equation*}
costs time exponential in the length of the input.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{P@$\P$|)}





% ===========================================
\section{$\NP$}\index{NP@$\NP$|(}
Recall that a machine is nondeterminstic\index{nondeterminism!for Turing machines}\index{Turing machine!nondeterminism} 
if
from a present configuration and input
it may pass to a next configuration with zero, or one, or more, next states.
Recall also that we have two mental models of how these devices operate.
In the first, the machine is unboundedly parallel:~its 
computation history is a tree and it computes all of the branches.
This
fits with our experience on everyday computers where, for instance,
we may have lots of windows open at one time, running lots of processes.
In the second model the machine guesses which next state to follow,
or is told which one by an oracle, and
then deterministically checks that branch of the computation tree.

With the first model we say an input string is accepted if at least one of the 
branches on the tree is an accepting branch.
With the second model we say the input
is accepted if there is a sequences of guesses that the machine could
make, or a sequence of hints that the demon could produce,
that leads to an accepting state.
With those definitions, the two models are logically equivalent in that
they accept the same languages.

We have already considered nondeterminism for both Finite State machines
and Pushdown machines.
For Finite State machines, nondeterminism did not make any
difference in the sense that for any
job done by a nondeterministic Finite State machine there is a deterministic
Finite State machine that can do the same job.
But Pushdown machines are a different
story:~there are jobs that can be done by
a nondeterministic Pushdown machine that cannot be done by any
deterministic machine.
One such job is accepting the language of palindromes.

%<*lem:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>
\begin{lemma}
Deterministic and nondeterministic Turing machines solve the same problems.
In particular,
every problem that can be solved by a nondeterministic machine
can be solved by a deterministic machine.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>

%<*pf:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>
\begin{proof}
Since a deterministic Turing machine is a special case of a
nondeterministic one, one direction is obvious.

For the other, start with a nondeterministic machine and we will
describe how a deterministic machine can simulate the same computation.
The nondeterministic machine generates a computation tree.
The deterministic machine can do a breadth-first traversal of that tree.
If there is an accepting branch then the deterministic machine will
eventually reach it and it also will accept the input,
otherwise it will not accept the input.
\end{proof}
%</pf:DeterministicAndNondeterministicTMsDoTheSame>

That proof is, basically, time-slicing.
With the machines that are on our desks and in our pockets,
we simulate an unboundedly-parallel computer
by having the CPU switch among processes, giving each enough time to
make some progress but not so much time that other processes are
starved.
This is a form of the dovetailing that we have see earlier.
To the user it appears that many things
are happening at once but actually there is only one, or
at least a limited number of, simultaneous physical processes.\footnote{%
  Depending on how many CPU's the device has.}

So nondeterminism
doesn't add to what can be computed in principle.
But that doesn't mean that these machines are worthless.

For one thing, we saw with nondeterministic Finite State machines
that they can be a good fit for the problems we wanted to solve.
There, we could easily go from problem statement to a nondeterministic machine
and then get a deterministic machine from that.
We can do a similar thing with Turing machines.

Similarly, one interesting thing about
nondeterministic Turing machines is that they
appear to be a good match for solving some problems that on a serial device
are hard to solve.
An example is the Travelling Salesman problem.
Stated in guessing terms, the machine simply
guesses, making a sequence of where-next guesses, to find the best circuit.
Or, in supernatural terms, 
the machine is given a circuit by some oracular demon 
and then checks whether it is shorter than the given bound.
So for some problems, nondeterminism simplifies going from the problem statement
to a solution statement.


% ..................
\subsection{Speed}
But the real excitement is that 
a nondeterministic Turing machine, if we had one,
might be much faster than a deterministic one.

%<*discussion:SpeedOfNFSMi>
\begin{example}  \label{ex:SatisfiabilityfastOnNDTM}
Consider \probname{Satisfiability}.\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem!on a nondeterministic Turing machine}
Is this propositional logic formula satisfiable?
\begin{equation*}
 f = 
 (P\vee Q)\wedge (P\vee \neg Q)\wedge (\neg P\vee Q)
  \wedge (\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R) \wedge (Q\vee R)
\end{equation*} 
The natural approach is to compute a truth table
and see whether the final column has any $T$'s.
Here the next to last row has the~$T$ so this formula is satisfiable.
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\vee Q$ &$P\vee \neg Q$ &$\neg P\vee Q$ &$\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R$ &$Q\vee R$ &$f$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{example}
%</discussion:SpeedOfNFSMi>
%<*discussion:SpeedOfNFSMii>
The number of rows in a truth table grows  
exponentially with the number of variables, 
specificially it is $2$ raised to the number of variables.
So that approach is very slow, super-polynomial.

On the other hand,
this problem seems perfectly suited for unbounded parallelism.
For each line of the table we could fork a child process.
These children are done quickly, certainly in polytime.
Then we just call down the bus, ``Any successes?''
and if any child is holding a `$T$' then we declare $f$ satisfiable.
(In terms of the mental model:~the machine guesses a solution, 
or is given a solution by the demon, and
only has to verify that it works, which is also fast.)
In total then, a nondeterministic machine does this job in polytime
while it at least appears that a serial machine takes
exponential time.
%</discussion:SpeedOfNFSMii>

%<*def:NFSMAcceptsLang>
\begin{definition}\index{language!of a nondeterministic Turing machine}\index{nondeterministic Turing machine!language}
A nondeterministic machine
\definend{decides the language~$\lang$ in time $\map{t}{\N}{\N}$} 
if for any
input $\sigma\in\lang$ the machine computes at least one accepting branch
in time $t(\mspace{2mu}|\sigma|\mspace{2mu})$. % \footnotemark[2].
\end{definition} % \footnotetext[2]{Whether we use the model that the machine
  % decides by a computation tree or by guessing does not matter.}
%</def:NFSMAcceptsLang>

So while adding unbounded parallelism to Turing machines doesn't
allow them to compute any entirely new functions, a person could
sensibly conjecture that it may
allow them to compute some of functions faster.




\subsection{Definition}
We now name the class of problems associated with nondeterministic 
Turing machines.
% We will give two equivalent descriptions, \cref{def:NP} and 
% \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}.

%<*def:NP>
\begin{definition} \label{def:NP}
The complexity 
class~\definend{$\compclass{NP}$}\index{NP@$\NP$} 
is the set of languages for which there is a 
a polynomial time nondeterministic Turing machine acceptor.
\end{definition}
%</def:NP>

%<*lem:PSubsetNP>
\begin{lemma}
$\P\subseteq\NP$
\end{lemma}
%</lem:PSubsetNP>

%<*pf:PSubsetNP>
\begin{proof}
Any deterministic Turing machine is trivially also a nondeterministic one.
So a problem that has a polynomial time algorithm on a deterministic
machine trivially has a polytime algorithm on a
nondeterministic machine.
\end{proof}
%</pf:PSubsetNP>

%<*rem:DunnoPvsNP>
\begin{remark}\index{P versus NP@$\P$ versus $\NP$}
Very important:~no one knows whether $\P$ is a strict subset, 
so that $\P\neq\NP$, or whether $\P=\NP$.
That is, at this moment no one knows whether, because
deterministic machines do not come with unbounded parallelism baked in
and must simulate it with time-slicing,
they are necessarily slower.
We will take up this question again in the next section.
\end{remark}
%</rem:DunnoPvsNP>

We next see an alternate  way to show that a 
problem is in~$\NP$.\footnote{%
  A common pattern in mathematical presentations is to have a
  a definition that is conceptually clear and 
  to follow it with results that make it easier to 
  compute whether the definition applies.}

%<*intro:Verifier>
Consider the above instance of \probname{Satisfiability},
\cref{ex:SatisfiabilityfastOnNDTM}.
Each line of the truth table is easy; the challenge 
is that there are a lot of lines.
Intuitively, we can conjecture that 
a nondeterministic machine can use 
unbounded parallelism to simultaneously cover all the lines. 
Switching to the other way that we conceptualize nondeterminism,
if the demon whispers, 
``Psst! Look at $\omega=\str{TTF}$.'' 
then a deterministic machine could quickly verify 
that the formula is satisfiable. 
%</intro:Verifier>
 
%<*def:Verifier>
\begin{definition}  \label{def:Verifier}
For a language, 
a deterministic Turing machine~$\TM$ is a
\definend{verifier} if 
for every $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$, that string is member of the language
if and only if
there exists a \definend{witness} or \definend{certificate}
string $\omega\in\kleenestar{\B}$ such that
$\TM$ accepts~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
\end{definition}
%</def:Verifier>

%<*lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv>
\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
A language is a member of $\NP$ 
if and only
if it has a polynomial time verifier.
That is, $\lang\in\NP$ if and only if there is a 
deterministic Turing machine~$\TM$ so that
for every
$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$ there is a witness~$\omega$
where $\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $\TM$ accepts 
$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, in time polynomial in~$\sigma$.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv>

So to show that a language is in~$\NP$ we write a verifier~$\TM$, 
a program that inputs hints~$\omega$ and confirms that they work.  
Before the proof we will see some examples and discuss some aspects of the 
definition and lemma.

%<*ex:SATIsNPi>
\begin{example}
The \probname{Satisfiability}\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem} 
problem is to determine membership in this
language.
\begin{equation*}
  \SAT=
  \set{\sigma\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ represents a propositional logic formula~$f$ that is satisfiable}}
\end{equation*}
For the witnesses we can use strings pointing to a line of the truth table,
such as $\omega=\str{TTF}$.
This family of witnesses works because we can write
the verifier shown below, a Turing machine~$\TM$ that inputs
a representation of a propositional logic statement
$\sigma$ and a representation of a truth table line~$\omega$,
and accepts that pair if it can confirm that on that line 
the statement~$\sigma$ returns~$T$.
%</ex:SATIsNPi>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts000.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*ex:SATIsNPii>
If the statement~$\sigma$ is satisfiable then such an~$\omega$ exists,
and the machine can verify the truth-table line quickly, certainly in polytime.
If~$\sigma$ is not satisfiable then it doesn't matter what~$\omega$ 
the machine inputs, it will not be able to verify a~$T$ for that line.
So $\sigma\in\SAT$ if and only if there exists an~$\omega$ 
such that the verifier~$\TM$ accepts
$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
\end{example}
%</ex:SATIsNPii>

\begin{remark}
Before more examples we pause for a couple of comments.
The most striking thing about \cref{def:Verifier} is that it says 
`there exists' a witness~$\omega$.
It does not say where the witness comes from.
A person with programming experience may well ask, 
``but how will we compute~$\omega$?'' 
The question is not how to find it.
The question is instead:~can we write a verifier program that
takes~$\omega$'s, say from a demon,
and uses them to verify that the $\sigma$'s are in $\lang$, in polytime?

A second point is that the definition does not require that there be a 
witness in the case that $\sigma\notin\lang$.
Instead, in this case there just is no witness~$\omega$ 
for the verifier to accept.

Third, a comment on the running time of the verifier.
Contrast the above example with 
\citetext{the problem of chess}{ 
  Chess is known to be a solvable game.
  This is Zermelo's Theorem \autocite{wiki:ZermelosTheorem}\Dash there 
  is a strategy for one of the two players that forces a win or a draw, no
  matter how the opponent plays}.
Imagine that a demon hands you some papers and tells you
that they contain a perfect chess strategy, a flowchart that if you follow it
then you cannot lose.
Verifying that this strategy works, by having a computer step through
the responses to each move and responses to those
responses, etc., 
\citetext{at least appears to be an exponential-time job}{%
  Chess is known to be EXP complete.}.
So at least at first glance, chess does not seem to be 
verifiable in polytime.
Thus, the fact that the definition requires that 
any verifier runs in time polynomial in $\lh{\sigma}$  
makes that verification tractable.

In addition, because the verifier runs in time polynomial in $\lh{\sigma}$,
the witness~$\omega$ must have length polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.
This is simply because if 
the witness were too long then the
machine would not even be able to read it in the alotted time.

Finally, above we described $\sigma$ as a string `representing' 
a propositional logic statement
and $\omega$ as `representing' a table line.
That gets tedious.
\citetext{In practice authors usually omit it}{%
  This is like a working programmer saying ``My program
  inputs a number,'' rather than saying ``My program inputs the
  binary representation of a number.''
  It is also like a person saying ``That's me on the card'' rather
  than ``On that card is a picture of me.''}
and from now on we'll do the same.
For instance, the next example says that $\sigma$ is a triple instead of 
saying that it represents a triple. 
\end{remark}

%<*ex:HamPathIsNPi>
\begin{example}
The \definend{\probname{Hamiltonian Path}} problem is like
\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} except that it inputs two vertices 
instead of requiring that the starting vertex equal the ending one.  
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}\suchthat 
             \text{some path in~$\mathcal{G}$ between $v$ and~$\hat{v}$ visits every vertex}}
\end{equation*}
We will show that this problem is in~$\NP$.
To do that, we will describe a family of witness~$\omega$'s, hints from the
demon that will be inputs for a
suitable Turing machine verifier~$\TM$.
We take the witness to be a path, 
$\omega=\sequence{v,v_1,\ldots\, \hat{v}}$.
Our verifier inputs~$\sigma=\sequence{\mathcal{G},v,\hat{v}}$ 
and~$\omega$ and then it
tries to confirm that the list of vertices visited by the path is all
of the vertices in the graph.
%</ex:HamPathIsNPi>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts001.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*ex:HamPathIsNPii>
If there is a Hamiltonian path for the graph and vertices then there exists
a witness~$\omega$ leading to acceptance by~$\TM$ of the pair (the
check can be done in polytime).
If there is no such path then for no~$\omega$ is~$\TM$  
able to verify, so it never accepts.
\end{example}
%</ex:HamPathIsNPii>

%<*ex:CompositeIsNP>
\begin{example}
\probname{Composite} asks whether a number has a nontrivial factor.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{n\in\N\suchthat \text{$n$ has a divisor~$a$ with $1<a<n$}}
\end{equation*}
For a witness~$\omega$ we can use any nontrivial factor~$a$.
The verifier inputs~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ where $\sigma$ is a number~$n$.
If $\sigma\in\lang$ then the number is composite and there is a suitable 
witness, which the verifier can check in polytime.
If $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no suitable witness and the verifier will
never accept the input pair.
\end{example}
%</ex:CopositeIsNP>

\begin{example}
Recall \probname{Subset Sum}:~given a set of numbers $S\subset\N$ 
and a target sum~$T\in\N$,
we want to know if at least one subset of~$S$ adds to~$T$.
Recast it as the decision problem for
%\begin{equation*}
$
  \lang=\set{\sequence{S,T}\suchthat
     \text{a subset of $S$ totals to $T$}}
$.
%\end{equation*}
As the witness~$\omega$ we can use the subset~$\hat{S}\subseteq S$ 
whose members add to the target.
Verifying that the subset adds to the target in polynomial time is easy.
\end{example}

We finish with the promised proof
of \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivi>
Suppose first that the language~$\lang$ is accepted by the nondeterministic
Turing machine~$\TM$ in polynomial time;
we will construct a polynomial time verifier~$\TM[V]$.
Let $\map{p}{\N}{\N}$ be the polynomial 
such that the time taken by~$\TM$ on input~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$
is $p(|\sigma|)$.
For any $\sigma\in\lang$ the computation tree has an accepting branch.
We can make a certificate $\omega$ of the branchings.
With the certificate, a deterministic verifier can retrace $\TM$'s
accepting branch.
Because
the branch's length must be less than the length of the computation,
$p(|\sigma|)$,
the certificate will have length less than $p(|\sigma|)$.
So the verifier~$\TM[V]$ can do the retracing in polynomial time.
%</pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivi>

%<*pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivii>
For the other direction suppose that the language $\lang$
is accepted by a verifier~$\TM[V]$ that runs in time
that is bounded by some polynomial~$p$.
We will describe a process in guessing terms.
Given $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$, produce a sequence of $|\sigma|$-many
guesses of branches in the computation tree to make a witness string~$\omega$.
Use this witness along with $\sigma$ as inputs to $\TM[V]$.
Because of the verifier step, there is a way for this guessing process to
succeed if and only if $\sigma\in\lang$.
Because the verifier runs in polynomial time, the entire process of guessing and
then verifying does also.
%</pf:VerifierAndGuesserEquivii>
\end{proof}

So one way to think of $\NP$ is that it is the class of problems that 
have efficient verifiers:~there is a polytime algorithm that,
given a witness~$\omega$,
can use it to verify membership in the language.

In summary, we are interested in knowing for which problems
are there relatively good algorithms and for which are there not.
Cobham's Thesis identifies the problems
that have a good algorithm as the problems in~$\P$.
In this section we defined the class of problems for which
there is a good way to verify a solution, in contrast with the problems
for which there is a good algorithm to generate a solution.
In the next section we will consider whether these two classes differ.



% ........................
\begin{exercises}
% \begin{exercise}  \label{exer:PrimaliltyTesting}
% Express primality testing in computation tree terms.
% \end{exercise}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your friend says, ``$\NP$ is the set of problems with deterministic 
verifiers.''
That's not quite right;
gently correct them.
\begin{answer}
It matters that the verifier is both deterministic and runs in 
time polynomial in the length of the input.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Decide if each formula is satisfiable.
\begin{exparts}
\item $(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$
\item $(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This formula is satisfiable. 
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg Q$ &$\neg Q\wedge R$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item This formula is not satisfiable. 
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\to Q$ &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg P$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P$ &$\neg(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  &$(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}

\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % https://math.stackexchange.com/q/49509
A friend of yours asks, 
``In \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}, since the witness~$\omega$ is not
required to be effectively computable, 
why can't I just take it to be the bit~\str{1} if
$\sigma\in\lang$ and \str{0} if not?
Then writing the Turing machine is easy:~just ignore $\sigma$ and follow
the bit.''
They are confused.
Straighten them out.
\begin{answer}
That lemma requires that there be a verifier with the property 
that if $\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a witness leading to acceptance, 
and if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there exists no witness leading to 
acceptance.
Consider the problem $\lang=\set{n\in\N\suchthat \text{$n$ is even}}$.
Your proposed verifier inputs a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$,
where $\sigma$ is a number and $\omega$ is a bit, and then
accepts the pair if $\omega=\str{1}$.
Absolutely, if you give your verifier the pair  $\sequence{5,\str{1}}$
then it will not accept.
However, 
give it $\sequence{5,\str{1}}$ and your verifier will accept.
That is, for $\sigma=5$ there exists an~$\omega$ leading to acceptance, 
so your verifier is wrong.
Rather, for a correct verifier you need that there exists no~$\omega$ that 
causes it to accept $\sigma=5$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Sketch a nondeterminstic algorithm for each.
State it both in terms of a computation tree and in terms of guessing.
\begin{exparts}
\item
\probname{Three Dimensional Matching}:~where $X,Y,Z$ are sets of integers
having $n$ elements, given as input $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$ then
decide if there is an $n$-element $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ so that
no two triples agree on any coordinates.
\item
\probname{Partition}: given a finite multiset~$A$ such that each of 
its elements has a size $s(a)\in\N^+$,
decide if the set splits into 
$\hat{A},A-\hat{A}$ so the total of the sizes is the same,
$\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}s(a)=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}s(a)$.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Sketch a nondeterministic algorithm to solve each problem.
Give both a computation tree formulation and a guessing formulation.
\begin{exparts}
% \item

\probname{Prime Factorization}: given a number $n\in\N$, produce the prime
factorization.

The problem of \probname{$k$-Coloring}:~given an undirected graph,

\item 
The \definend{\probname{Marriage}} problem:~given equal numbers of men 
and women, each person ranks
the members of the other sex.
We must then assign to each person a partner.
An assignment is unstable if there are two people of opposite sex would
rather have each other than their assigned partners,
otherwise that assignment is stable.
\item
The \probname{Integer Linear Programming} problem:~maximize 
a linear objective function
$f(x_0,\ldots x_n)=d_0x_0+\cdots+d_nx_n$ subject to constraints

Thus, we start by fixing a permutation of the letters, for example

\begin{exercise}
True or False: for the
language~$\set{\sequence{a,b,c}\in\N^3\suchthat a+b=c}$,
the problem of deciding membership 
is in $\compclass{NP}$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Describe the problem of determining whether a number is odd as the problem
of deciding membership in a language.
Show it is in~$\NP$.
\begin{answer}
The natural way to describe it as a decision problem for a language
is with
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ ends in a \str{1}}}$
To show it is in $\NP$, we can have a deterministic Turing machine
that uses an empty witness $\omega=\emptystring$, and accepts such
strings in polynomial time.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Recall how we recast \probname{Travelling Salesman}
as a language decision problem by using the bound technique.
Show that the result is in $\NP$ by sketching a verifier that works with
a suitable witness.
\begin{answer}
We recast it as a language decision problem 
by setting a bound~$B\in\N$ and considering this set of bitstrings.
\begin{equation*}
  \TS_B=\set{\bar{G}\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
    \text{$G$ is a weighted graph with a circuit of length less than~$B$}}
\end{equation*}

To show that this problem is in~$\NP$ we must produce a verifier.
For a witness we can use a circuit 
$\omega=\sequence{v_{i_0},v_{i_1},\ldots v_{i_k}}$ that
is of total cost less than the bound.
The verifier gets an input pair~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ where 
$\sigma$ is a weighted graph, and checks 
that $\omega$ is indeed a Hamiltonian circuit of the graph, and that it 
costs less than the bound.
A verifier can do those checks in time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.
If the graph $\sigma$ is indeed an element of~$\TS_B$ then there is 
such an~$\omega$, so the verifier can accept a pair.
If $\sigma$ is not an element of~$\TS_B$ then there is no such 
$\omega$, and so the verifier will never accept such a pair.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The problem \probname{Double-$\SAT$}\index{Double-SAT@\probname{Double-$\SAT$} problem} 
is:~given a propositional logic statement,
decide whether it has at least two different substitutions of Boolean values
that make the statement true.
Show that this problem is in~$\NP$ by producing a suitable witness.
\begin{answer}
Given the propositional logic statement~$F$, a suitable witness is the
pair $\omega\sequence{\ell_0,\ell_1}$ consisting of two lines of the
table.
That is, each $\ell_i$ is a substitution, a map from the 
propositional variables $P,Q,R,\ldots$ to the set~$\set{T,F}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The problem of \probname{Independent Sets} starts with a graph and 
a natural number~$n$ and
decides whether in the graph there are $n$-many independent vertices,
that is, vertices that are not connected.
Show that this problem is in~$\NP$.
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Outline a nondeterministic algorithm that inputs a planar graph and outputs
a four-coloring.
Describe it both in terms of computation tree and in terms of a demon
giving the machine a witness.
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Check that \probname{Knapsack} is in~$\NP$ by checking that for
every `yes' there is a polynomial-length witness that can be verified
by a deterministic machine in polytime.
\begin{answer}
Let the given set be $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$.
Then there is a $k$-bit witness~$\omega$, where $\omega[i]=1$ if $s_i$ is in
the knapsack, and $\omega[i]=0$ if not.
Clearly that's polynomial in the length of the input, the bitstring
representing~$k$.

What remains is to check that the witness can be verified in polynomial
time.
This is just a matter of adding the weights associated with those $i$ such
that $\omega[i]=1$ to see that the total is below the weight bound,
and of adding the values to see that the total exceeds the value target.
Clearly this can be done in polytime.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Each of these problems is in~$\compclass{NP}$.
For each, give a witness.
\begin{exparts}
\item
The problem of finding is a given number is divisible by four.
\item
The problem of finding the product of two matrices.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

Give a witness for each of these problems from~$\compclass{NP}$.
