\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}
\item
\probname{Composite}
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Recast this as a decision problem and then show it is in~$\NP$:
\textit{\citetext{We are going to put numbers into boxes.}{%
    From \autocite{blog:ComputationalComplexityBoxesProblem}.} 
  If three numbers $x$,$y$, and~$z$ are in a box then it cannot be the case 
  that any two of them add to the third. 
  If you put all of the numbers $1,2,\ldots{} n$ into boxes, 
  what is the smallest number of boxes that you will need?}
\begin{answer}
To recast it as a language decision problem we produce a matching language.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sequence{n,k}\suchthat
        \text{there is a partition of $\set{1,2,\ldots{} n}$ into at most~$k$ boxes where no two members add to a third}}
\end{equation*}
The witness~$\omega$ is the partition, the set of boxes.
Checking that each box satisfies that no two members add to a third is clearly 
polytime. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each problem, recast it as a language decision problem by describing
the associated language, and then show it is in~$\NP$ by finding a 
witness.
\begin{exparts}
\item
The \definend{\probname{Linear Divisibilty}} problem inputs a pair of 
natural numbers $\sigma=\sequence{a,b}$ and asks
if there is an $x\in\N$ with $ax+1=b$.
\item
Given $n$ points scattered on a line, how far they are from each other
defines an $n(n+1)/2$-member multiset. 
The reverse of this problem, starting with the distances and 
reconstructing the positions, is the \definend{\probname{Turnpike}} problem.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
Here is the recasting, as the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sequence{a,b}\in\N^2\suchthat
        \text{there exists $x\in\N$ with $ax+1=b$}}
\end{equation*}
It is a member of~$\NP$ because we can take the witness to be $\omega=x$.
Checking that $ax+1=b$ is clearly polynomial.
\item
This is the equivalent language.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{M\suchthat 
        \text{$M$ is a multiset with $n(n+1)/2$ distances}}
\end{equation*}
The witness~$\omega$ is a set of suitable positions.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is the \HP{} in $\NP$?
\begin{answer}
The \HP{} is not decidable so it is not in $\NP$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Two graphs $\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1$ are isomorphic if there is a
one-to-one and onto function $\map{f}{\mathcal{V}_0}{\mathcal{V}_1}$ such
that $\set{v,\hat{v}}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_0$ if and only if
$\set{f(v),f(\hat{v})}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_1$.
Consider the problem of computing wheter two graphs are isomorphic.
\begin{exparts}
\item Define the appropriate language.
\item Show that the problem of determining membership in that language
  is a member of the class $\NP$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This is the natural language
$\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1}\suchthat\text{they are isomorphic}}$.
\item
The witness is the function, the set of ordered pairs $\sequence{v,f(v)}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% ==============================================
\section{Problem reduction}\index{problem reduction|(}
% "Reduction decreases the number of problems without increasing the number of
% answers"
When we studied incomputability we considered whether there could be a 
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$ routine that, given~$x$,
decides whether Turing machine~$x$ halts on input~$3$.
We showed that with such a checker we could solve the \HP, and
denoted that with 
$K\leq_T\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$.
The intuition behind the notation is that solving the 
Halts-on-three problem is at least
as hard as solving the \HP.
Formally, we write $B\leq_T A$ if there is a computable function~$f$ such that
$x\in B$ if and only if $f(x)\in A$ and we
then say that~$B$ is Turing-reducible to~$A$,\index{Turing reducible}
because to solve~$B$ it suffices to solve~$A$.
An instance is that the \HP{} is Turing-reducible 
to the Halts-on-three problem. 

In general, we say that problem $B$ is reduces to problem~$A$
if we can answer questions about~$B$ by accessing
answers to questions about~$A$.
A reduction is thus a way to translate from one to the other. 
The translation uses arbitrary computable functions for $\leq_T$,
and in other contexts uses other types of functions.

In computational complexity we want to solve problems efficiently.
In this context the appropriate idea of reduction, of some
version of ``$\lang_1\leq\lang_0$'' expressing that
$\lang_0$ is harder than~$\lang_0$,    
is that~$\lang_1\leq\lang_0$ if a method for solving~$\lang_0$ efficiently
gives us a method to solve~$\lang_1$ efficiently.

For instance, if we have an efficient algorithm that solves
\probname{Shortest Path} then with it we can efficiently solve  
\probname{Vertex-to-vertex Path},
simply by throwing away the path length information.
So \probname{Vertex-to-vertex Path} reduces to \probname{Shortest Path};~we 
shall write 
$\text{\probname{Vertex-to-vertex Path}}\leq_p\text{\probname{Shortest Path}}$.

%<*def:PolyTimeReduction>
\begin{definition}
Let $\lang_0,\lang_1$ be languages, subsets of~$\kleenestar{\B}$.
Then 
\definend{$\lang_1$ is polynomial time reducible to $\lang_0$}\index{polynomial time reducibility}\index{reducibility!polynomial time}
(or \definend{polynomial time mapping reducible}, or
\definend{polynomial time many-one reducible}, or
\definend{Karp reducible}),\index{Karp reducible} written 
\definend{$\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$},
if there is a computable function
$\map{f}{\kleenestar{\B}}{\kleenestar{\B}}$ that runs in polynomial time
such that
$\sigma\in\lang_1$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$.
\end{definition}
%</def:PolyTimeReduction>

%<*lem:PolytimeReductionTrans>
\begin{lemma}
If $\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ and $\prob_0\in\P$ then $\prob_1\in\P$. 
\end{lemma}
%</lem:PolytimeReductionTrans>

%<*pf:PolytimeReductionTrans>
\begin{proof}
Suppose that 
$\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ via the function~$f$ 
and also suppose that  
there is an algorithm for determining membership in~$\lang_0$.
To determine membership in $\lang_1$ we can do this:~given
input $\sigma$, find $f(\sigma)$ and apply the $\lang_0$-algorithm
to determine if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$.
Where the $\lang_0$ algorithm runs in time that is $\bigOh(n^i)$,
and where $f$ runs in polytime for a polynomial that has degree~$j$,
then determining $\lang_1$ membership in this way 
runs in time that is~$\bigOh(n^{\max(i,j)})$.
\end{proof}
%</pf:PolytimeReductionTrans>

\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeanWithReductions]{The bean encloses all problems.
  The ones with fast algorithms are at the bottom.
  Problems are connected if the one underneath reduces to the
  one above.}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp00.pdf}}
\end{fig}


\begin{example}
We will sketch an argument that \probname{Graph Colorability}
reduces to \probname{Satisfiability},
that $\probname{Graph Colorability}\leq_p\probname{Satisfiability}$.
We'll go through the $k=3$ construction; other $k$'s go the same way.

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATi>
Recall that a graph is $k$-colorable if we can partition the vertices
into $k$~many classes, called `colors' because that's how they
are pictured, so that there is no edge between two same-colored
vertices.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATi>
\begin{animatedfigure}{A $3$-coloring of \protect\citetext{a graph}{This is the Petersen graph, a rich source of counterexamples for conjectures in Graph Theory}.}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=last]{2}{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity}{00}{01}
\end{animatedfigure} 
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATii>
\noindent And, a propositional logic statement~$S$ is satisfiable if there is an
assignment of the variables in the statement that make the statement
as a whole evaluate to~$T$.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATii>

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiii>
Write the set of all graphs as $\lang_1$ and
write the set of propositional logic statements as~$\lang_0$.
(As discussed earlier, these are actually sets of bit strings.
One is the set of strings  
$\gamma\in\lang_1$ representing graphs~$\mathcal{G}$ 
with reasonable efficiency.
The other is the set of reasonably efficient string representations 
$\sigma\in\lang_0$ of statements~$S$.
For the rest of the discussion we will not mention representations.)

To show that $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ we will produce a 
translation function~$f$
that inputs a graph~$\mathcal{G}$
and a outputs a propositional logic statement $S=f(\mathcal{G})$
such that if the graph is $3$-colorable 
if and only if the statement is satisfiable.
The function must run in
polynomial time.

Let~$\mathcal{G}$ have vertices $\set{v_0,\ldots\, v_{n-1}}$.
The statement will have $3n$-many Boolean variables:
$
  {a_0,\ldots a_{n-1}}
$, and
$  
  {b_0,\ldots b_{n-1}}
$, and
$  
  {c_0,\ldots c_{n-1}}
$.
The idea is that if the $i$-th vertex~$v_i$ gets the first color then 
the associated variable $a_i$ evaluates to True, $a_i=T$,
while if it gets the second color then $b_i=T$, and if it gets the
third color then $c_i=T$.
Thus, for
each vertex~$v_i$ create a clause saying that it gets at least one color.
\begin{equation*}
  (a_i\vee b_i \vee c_i)
\end{equation*}
In addition, for each edge~$\set{v_i,v_j}$
create three clauses that together ensure that the edge does not connect
two same-color vertices.
\begin{equation*}
  (\neg a_i\vee \neg a_j)
  \qquad
  (\neg b_i\vee \neg b_j)
  \qquad
  (\neg c_i\vee \neg c_j)
\end{equation*}
Now the statement is the conjunction of those clauses;
it joins them with $\wedge$'s.
The graph has a $3$-coloring if and only if that statement
is satisfiable.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiii>

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiv>
This illustrates.
The graph has four vertices so the statement starts with four clauses,
saying that for each vertex~$v_i$ at least one of the associated
variables $a_i$, $b_i$, or~$c_i$ has the value~$T$.
The graph has four edges, $v_0v_1$, $v_0v_3$, $v_1v_2$, and
$v_2v_3$.
The statement continues with three clauses for each edge, 
together ensuring that the variables associated with the edge's vertices
do not both have the value~$T$.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiv>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems23.pdf}}
  \hspace{1.5em}
  \begin{minipage}{3.25in}\small
    \begin{multline*}
      S=(a_0\vee b_0 \vee c_0)\wedge  
      (a_1\vee b_1 \vee c_1)\wedge  
      (a_2\vee b_2 \vee c_2)\wedge  
      (a_3\vee b_3 \vee c_3)             \\
      \begin{split}
        &\wedge (\neg a_0\vee\neg a_1)
               \wedge (\neg b_0\vee\neg b_1)
               \wedge (\neg c_0\vee\neg c_1)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_0\vee\neg a_3)
               \wedge (\neg b_0\vee\neg b_3)
               \wedge (\neg c_0\vee\neg c_3)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_1\vee\neg a_2)
               \wedge (\neg b_1\vee\neg b_2)
               \wedge (\neg c_1\vee\neg c_2)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_1\vee\neg a_3)
               \wedge (\neg b_1\vee\neg b_3)
               \wedge (\neg c_1\vee\neg c_3)  
      \end{split}
    \end{multline*}
  \end{minipage}
\end{center}
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATv>
Thus, $S$ is satisfiable if and only if $\mathcal{G}$ has a $3$-coloring.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATv>
  
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvi>
Completing the proof means
checking that the translation function, which inputs a bit string
representation of~$\mathcal{G}$ and outputs a bitstring represenation
of~$S$, is polymomial.
That's clear, although the argument is messy so we'll skip it.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvi>
\end{example}

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvii>
The significance of the reduction is that we now know that
if we could solve~\probname{Satisfiability} 
in polynomial time then we could solve
\probname{$k$ Colorability} in polynomial time.
Given a $k$-coloring problem~$\mathcal{G}$, translate it to a
a $\SAT$ problem~$f(\mathcal{G})=S$.
The translation takes polytime and~$\lang_1$ takes polytime, so
in total this is a polytime scheme.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvii>

That example give some sense of why $\SAT$ can be a convenient
problem to reduce to, a benchmark problem,
since describing the conditions in a problem
in logical statement terms is natural.
Another kind of natural reduction is when we can see that one problem
as a special case of another, or at least closely related.

\begin{example}
We will show that $\probname{Subset Sum}\leq_p\probname{Knapsack}$,
so that a quick way to solve the Knapsack problem would
yield a quick way to solve the Subset Sum problem.

Recall that \probname{Subset Sum} 
starts with a multiset $S=\set{b_0,\ldots b_{k-1}}\subset\N$
and a target~$T\in\N^+$.
It asks if there is a subset whose elements add to the target.

\probname{Knapsack} starts with a multiset
of objects~$\hat{S}=\set{s_0, \ldots s_{n-1}}$, along with a bound $W\in\N$
and a target $V\in\N$.
Each~$s_i$ has both a weight~$w(s_i)\in\N^+$ and a value~$v(s_i)\in\N^+$.
The problem is to decide if there is subset $K\subseteq S$
such that the sum of its element weights is less than or equal to~$W$
while the sum of the values is greater than or equal to~$V$.

The reduction function~$f$ inputs instances of \probname{Subset Sum}, 
pairs $\sequence{S,T}$, and outputs instances of 
\probname{Knapsack}, five-tuples $\sequence{\hat{S},v,V,w,W}$,
and must run in polytime.
For the reduction, convert to the special case of \probname{Knapsack} 
where each element
has the same weight as value, $w(s_i)=v(s_i)$,
and where $W=V=T$.

As an illustration, suppose that we 
want to know if there is a subset of
$S=\set{18, 23, 31, 33, 72,  86, 94}$ that adds to $T=126$.
Let the \probname{Knapsack} instance $f(S)=\hat{S}$ 
have the same number of elements as $S$, so $\hat{S}= \set{s_0,\ldots s_{6}}$,
let $w(s_0)=v(s_0)=18$, $w(s_1)=v(s_1)=23$, etc.,
and set the weight and value targets $W,V$ to be~$T=126$, 
% as in the table below.
% \begin{center}
% \begin{tabular}{r|*{7}{c}}
%   \tabulartext{object} &$s_0$ &$s_1$ &$s_2$ &$s_3$ &$s_4$ &$s_5$ &$s_6$  \\
%   \cline{2-8}
%   \rule{0pt}{11pt}
%   \tabulartext{weight} &$18$  &$23$  &$31$  &$33$  &$72$  &$86$ &$94$  \\
%   \tabulartext{value}  &$18$  &$23$  &$31$  &$33$  &$72$  &$86$ &$94$  \\
% \end{tabular}
% \hspace{2em}
% $W=V=126$
% \end{center}
A \probname{Subset Sum} instance has a solution~$A\subseteq S$ if and only
the \probname{Knapsack} instance has a solution~$K=f(A)\subseteq\hat{S}$.
Showing that the function~$f$ to convert the bitstring representations
of problem instances is polytime is easy.
\end{example}

Above we showed that \probname{$k$ Coloring} reduces to 
\probname{Satisfiability}, so doing
\probname{Satisfiability} fast is at least as hard as doing 
\probname{$k$ Coloring} fast.
There, we commented that \probname{Satisfiability} is often convenient for
reductions and in the next section we will give a theorem saying that it
is at least as hard as every problem in $\NP$.
But we close by giving a problem that
is at least as hard as \probname{Satisfiability}.

\begin{example}
%<*ex:SATLeqCliquei>
Recall \probname{Clique}:~given a graph~$\mathcal{G}$ and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a $B$-clique,
a size~$B$ set of vertices such that every vertex in the set is connected to
every other vertex.
We will suggest how to construct the polytime function~$f$ giving that 
$\text{\probname{Satisfiability}}\leq_p\text{\probname{Clique}}$.

The reduction translates each \probname{Satisfiability} instance,
a propositional logic statement~$S$,
to a \probname{Clique} instance $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}=f(S)$.
Consider this example propositional logic statement,
$
  S=(x_0\vee x_1)\wedge(\neg x_0\vee\neg x_1)\wedge(x_0\vee \neg x_1)
$.
The three components $x_0\vee x_1$, $\neg x_0\vee\neg x_1$, and
$x_0\vee \neg x_1$ are called `clauses'.
In a clause, an atom is either a variable~$x_i$ or its negation~$\neg x_i$.
Put a vertex in $\mathcal{G}$
for each occurrence of an atom in a clause.
The statement~$S$ has three clauses with two atoms each
so the graph has six vertices, as shown below.
Put an edge in~$\mathcal{G}$ if the associated atoms 
are in different clauses
and are not negations (that is, not $x_i$ and $\neg x_i$).
% \begin{align*}
%   \mathcal{V} &=\set{v_{0,0},v_{0,1},v_{1,0},v_{1,1},v_{2,0},v_{2,1}} \\
%   \mathcal{E} &=\{\,\set{v_{0,0},v_{2,0}},\,
%                   \set{v_{0,0},v_{1,1}},\,
%                   \set{v_{0,0},v_{2,1}},  \\
%               &\qquad \set{v_{0,1},v_{1,0}},\,
%                       \set{v_{0,1},v_{2,0}}, \\
%               &\qquad \set{v_{1,0},v_{2,0}},\,
%                       \set{v_{1,0},v_{2,1}}, \\
%               &\qquad \set{v_{1,1},v_{2,0}}\,\}
                  %   \end{align*}
This is the graph arising from $S$.
%</ex:SATLeqCliquei>
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity03.pdf}
\end{center}
%<*ex:SATLeqCliqueii>
Observe that 
$S$ is satisfiable if and only if
the graph has a $3$-clique.
Showing that the translation function~$f$ is polytime is routine.
%</ex:SATLeqCliqueii>
\end{example}



% ......................................
\subsection{$\compclass{NP}$ complete}\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete|(}
\margingraphic{0.17\textwidth}{complexity/pix/cook.jpg}{\label{fig:StephenCook}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Cook}{Stephen Cook b~1939}}\index{Cook, S!picture} % http://web.cs.toronto.edu/Assets/Computer+Science+Digital+Assets/cooknsercmed.jpg http://web.cs.toronto.edu/news/current/herzbergcook.htm
Because $\P\subseteq\NP$, the class $\NP$ contains lots of easy problems,
ones with a fast algorithm.
For instance, one member of $\NP$ is
the problem of determining whether a number is odd.
Of course, the interest in the class is that it also contains lots of
problems that seem to be hard.
Can we prove that those problems are indeed hard?

In 1971 this question was raised by S~Cook.
He noted the significance of polynomial time reducibility, so that
an efficient solution to one problem results in an efficient
solution for the other.
And, he
showed that among the problems in $\NP$,
there are ones that are maximally hard.\footnote{%
  This was also shown by L~Levin who was behind the Iron Curtain so
  that knowledge of his work did not spread west for some time.}

%<*th:CookLevin>
\begin{theorem}[Cook-Levin theorem]\index{Cook-Levin theorem}\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}
\probname{Satisfiability} has the property
that $\prob\leq_p\SAT$ for any $\prob\in\NP$.
\end{theorem}
%</th:CookLevin>

%<*pf:CookLevini>
First, $\SAT\in\NP$ because
a nondeterministic machine can guess which line of the truth
table to verify.
Said another way:~given a Boolean formula,
use as a certificate~$\omega$ a sequence giving an assignment of truth values
that satisfies the formula.
%</pf:CookLevini>

\margingraphic{0.18\textwidth}{complexity/pix/levin.png}{\label{fig:L Levin}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Levin}{Leonid Levin b~1948}} % https://www.bu.edu/today/2014/american-academy-of-arts-sciences-taps-cas-prof-for-membership/
%<*pf:CookLevinii>
The rest of the proof is long so it is in an Extra section.
But its basic idea is reachable.
We are given a problem $\prob\in\NP$ and we must show that
$\prob\leq_p\SAT$.
To show that $\prob$ is reducible to $\SAT$ we must translate
membership questions for $\prob$ into a Boolean
formulas so that the membership answer is `yes' if and
only if the formula evaluates to $T$.
The only thing we know about the problem $\prob$ is that its input
$\sigma$ is
accepted by some nondeterministic machine~$\TM$ in time given by
a polynomial~$p$.
So the proof constructs, from $\TM$, $\sigma$, and $p$,
a Boolean formula that yields $T$ if and only if
$\TM$ accepts~$\sigma$.
The Boolean formula encodes the constraints that a Turing machine
operates under, such as that the only tape symbol that can be changed in
any one step is the one under the machine's Read/Write head.
%</pf:CookLevinii>

Our main interest in the theorem is that it shows there are 
problems in $\NP$ that are at least as hard as any problem in
that class.

%<*def:NPHard>
\begin{definition}
A problem~$\prob$ is \definend{$\NP$-hard}\index{NP hard@$\NP$ hard}\index{hard!NP@$\NP$}
if $\hat{\prob}\leq_p\prob$ for every~$\hat{\prob}\in\compclass{NP}$.
A problem~$\prob$ is \citetext{\definend{$\NP$~complete}}{%
  The name came from a contest run by Knuth; see 
  \url{http://blog.computationalcomplexity.org/2010/11/by-any-other-name-would-be-just-as-hard.html}.}\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete}\index{complete!NP@$\NP$}
if it is $\NP$-hard and is a member of $\NP$.

In general, a problem~$\prob$ is 
\definend{hard for a class~\compclass{C}}\index{hard!for a class} if 
all problems in that class reduce to it:
if $\hat{\prob}\in\compclass{C}$ then $\hat{\prob}\leq_p\prob$.
A problem is 
\definend{complete for a class}\index{complete!for a class}
if it is hard for that
class and is also a member of that class.
\end{definition}
%</def:NPHard>

\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeanWithNPComplete]{This shows all problems,
  the collection of all $\lang\in\kleenestar{\B}$.
  In the bottom right is~$\NP$, drawn with $\P$ as
  a strict subset (although we don't know that is true).
  In the top right, shaded, are the $\NP$-hard problems.
  The highlighted intersection of the two
  is the set of $\NP$ complete problems.}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp06.pdf}}
\end{fig}

%<*lem:NPCompleteIsTransitive>
\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:InteractionNPCompleteAndReduction}
If $\prob_0$ is $\NP$~complete, and $\prob_0\leq_p\prob_1$,
and $\prob_1\in\NP$ then $\prob_1$ is $\NP$~complete.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:NPCompleteIsTransitive>

\begin{proof}
\Cref{exer:ProveInteractionNPCompleteAndReduction}.
\end{proof}

The $\NP$~complete problems are to the class~$\NP$ as the problems 
Turing-equivalent to~$K$,
the solution to the \HP, 
are to the computably enumerable sets.
They are at the top level of their class:~we could solve 
every other problem in that
class if we could solve that one problem.
(However, we know that we cannot solve~$K$.
The situation is different with the $\NP$~complete problems;
more below.)

\margingraphic{0.20\textwidth}{complexity/pix/karp.jpg}{\label{fig:RichardKarp}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_M._Karp}{Richard M.\@ Karp b~1935}}\index{Karp, R!picture} % https://simons.berkeley.edu/people/richard-karp  https://simons.berkeley.edu/sites/default/files/styles/profile_main/public/rk1.jpg?itok=1kn5XodZ
%<*discussion:KarpsProblems>
Soon after Cook raised the question of $\NP$~completeness, 
R~Karp brought it to widespread attention.
He produced a list of twenty one problems that were well known to be difficult,
ones where many smart people had for years
been unable find an efficient algorithm.
Karp showed that they were all $\NP$~complete and so
if any one could be solved by an efficient algorithm
then they all could be. % Based off Karp's Turing award statement http://amturing.acm.org/award_winners/karp_3256708.cfm
%</discussion:KarpsProblems>

Karp's paper made clear
that the collection of $\NP$~complete problems encompassed many problems,
spread across Computer Science, Mathematics, and the natural sciences.
These are hard problems, ones that researchers have struggled to find a 
fast algorithm to solve. 
Not every hard problem is $\NP$~complete but many,
many thousands of problems have been shown to be $\NP$~complete and so 
whatever it is that makes these problems hard, they all share it.
 
\citetext{The next list}{%
  These are from the standard, classic, reference \autocite{GareyJohnson}.}
contains the problems
most often used to
prove that something is $\NP$~complete.
These descriptions appeared earlier; they are copied
here for convenience.
Typically, proving that a problem~$\prob$ is $\NP$~complete has two halves.
The first half shows that~$\prob\in\NP$.
Usually this is easy, just show that a witness exists that could
be verified in polytime.
The second half shows that your problem is $\NP$-hard.
Often this involves showing that
some problem on the list reduces to~$\prob$; for instance, that 
$\text{$3$-$\SAT$}\leq_p\prob$.

%<*th:GJNPCompleteProblems>
\begin{theorem}[Basic $\compclass{NP}$ Complete Problems]\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete!basic problems}
Each of these problems is $\compclass{NP}$-complete.
\begin{thmdescription}
\item[\probname{$3$-Satisfiability}, $3$-$\SAT$]\index{3 SAT@$3$-$\SAT$}\index{3 Satisfiability@\probname{$3$-Satisfiability} problem}
Given a propositional logic formula in conjunctive normal form
in which each clause has at most $3$ variables,
decide if it is satisfiable.

\item[\probname{$3$ Dimensional Matching}]\index{3 Dimensional Matching@\probname{$3$ Dimensional Matching} problem}
Given as input a set $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$, where the sets $X,Y,Z$
all have the same number of elements, $n$,
decide if there is a matching,
a set $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ containing~$n$ elements such that no
two of the triples in $\hat{M}$ agree on any of their coordinates.

\item[\probname{Vertex cover}]\index{Vertex cover@\probname{Vertex cover} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a $B$-vertex cover,
a size~$B$ set of vertices~$C$
such that for any edge $v_iv_j$, at least one of its ends
is a member of~$C$.

\item[\probname{Clique}]\index{Clique@\probname{Clique} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a $B$-clique,
a set of $B$-many vertices such that any two are connected.

\item[\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}]\index{Hamiltonian Circuit@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}
Given a graph, decide if it contains a Hamiltonian circuit,
a cyclic path that includes each vertex.

\item[\probname{Partition}]\index{Partition@\probname{Partition} problem}
Given a finite multiset~$S$,
decide if there is a division of the set into two parts
$\hat{S}$ and $S-\hat{S}$ so the total of the elements in the two
is the same, $\sum_{s\in\hat{S}}s=\sum_{s\notin\hat{S}}s$.
\end{thmdescription}
\end{theorem}
%</th:GJNPCompleteProblems>

\begin{example}
%<*ex:TSIsNPComplete>
\probname{Travelling Salesman} is $\NP$ complete because
\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}
reduces to it:
$\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}\leq_p\probname{Travelling Salesman}$.
To prove this 
we must produce a reduction function~$f$ and check that it is polytime.

The function inputs an instance of \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}, a graph
$\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}$
whose edges are unweighted.
It returns
the instance of \probname{Travelling Salesman} that uses the vertex set
$\mathcal{V}$ as cities and that takes the distances between the cities
to be: $d(v_i,v_j)=1$ if $v_iv_j$ is an edge of $\mathcal{G}$ and
$d(v_i,v_j)=2$ if $v_iv_j\notin\mathcal{E}$,
and such that the bound~$B$ is the number of vertices~$|\mathcal{V}|$.

The bound means that there will be a \probname{Travelling Salesman} solution
if and only if there is a \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} solution, namely
the \probname{Travelling Salesman} solution uses the edges of 
the Hamiltonian circuit.
What remains is to show that $f$ is polytime in the size of the input problem
instance, which we take to be the number of vertices since the number of
edges is less than twice the number of vertices (so that polytime in the
number of vertices is also polytime in the number of vertices plus the
number of edges).
To output the \probname{Travelling Salesman} instance, the algorithm examines
all the pairs of vertices, which is a nested loop and so takes time that
is quadratic in the number of vertices.
%</ex:TSIsNPComplete>
\end{example}

One of Karp's points was the practical importance of
$\NP$~completeness.
Many problems from applications fall into this class.
The next example illustrates.
(It also illustrates that reductions can be complex.)

\begin{example} % From https://people.cs.umass.edu/~barring/cs311/disc/9.html
Most colleges make a schedule by putting classes into
time slots and then students pick which classes they will take.
Imagine instead that students first select the classes, and then the
college must solve the problem of deciding if there is a
non-conflicting time schedule for those classes.

Consider a college with $2$~time slots,
$k$-many classes, each with some capacity for enrolled students, and 
$n$-many total students.
Every student~$s$ has two disjoint lists of classes
$\ell_s,\hat{\ell}_s$ and
must choose one class from each list $c_s\in\ell_s$ and
$\hat{c}_s\in\hat{\ell}_s$.
The problem~$\prob$ is to decide if there is a way to partition the set
of classes into two such that every class is in a time slot and every
student is in both of the classes they selected, and no student is in
two classes in the same time slot.
We will show that $\prob$ is $\NP$~complete.

Observe that $\prob$ is a member of $\NP$ because checking a solution is
fast:~a witness~$\omega$ is a sequence of assignments of students to classes
and classes to time slots, which has length polynomial
in the number of students, and we can in polytime
check that each student is assigned one class from each of their lists,
that each class is given a time slot, etc.

We will show that $\prob$ is $\NP$-hard by showing that
$\text{$3$-$\SAT$}\leq_p\prob$, that
there is a reduction function that runs in polytime.
An input instance to this function
consists of $m$-many clauses that are joined by $\wedge$'s,
each clause with $3$ or fewer of the Boolean variables
$x_0$, \ldots $x_{n-1}$ or their negations,
joined by $\vee$'s.
The reduction function gives as output an instance of $\prob$.
The output instance will have a successful schedule if and only if the
input formula is satisfiable.

For each of the input instance's Boolean variables
the output instance will have an associated course: $c_0$, \ldots $c_{n-1}$
(of capacity equal to the total number of students).
There will be more courses also, including one named `T' and one named `F'
(the idea is that
if the input is satisfied by an assignment where $x_i=T$ then
the output can allot $c_i$ to the same slot as course~T).
These will each have capacity $2m+1$.
Also start by giving the output $\prob$ instance 
a student $s_0$ whose two lists are
$\ell_{s_0}={\text{T}}$ and $\hat{\ell}_{s_0}=\set{\text{F}}$; this
puts them in separate slots.

Now consider the clauses in the input formula.
The easiest clauses are the ones without negations, of the form
$x_{i_0}$, or $x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}$, or $x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}\vee x_{i_2}$.
If the clause is $x_{i_0}$ then in the output instance we create a student
$s$ with the two lists
$\ell_s=\set{c_{i_0}}$ and $\hat{\ell}_s=\set{\text{F}}$.
If the clause is $x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}$ then the student
gets
$\ell_s=\set{c_{i_0},c_{i_1}}$ and $\hat{\ell}_s=\set{\text{F}}$.
Similarly, for $x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}\vee x_{i_2}$ the created student's
lists are 
$\ell_s=\set{c_{i_0},c_{i_1},c_{i_2}}$ and $\hat{\ell}_s=\set{\text{F}}$.

Clauses with only negations, of the form
$\neg x_{i_0}$, or $\neg x_{i_0}\vee \neg x_{i_1}$,
or $\neg x_{i_0}\vee \neg x_{i_1}\vee \neg x_{i_2}$,
are similar.
For instance, for $\neg x_{i_0}$ we create a student with lists 
$\ell_s=\set{\text{T}}$ and $\hat{\ell}_s=\set{c_{i_0}}$.

The messiest clauses have a mixture of positive and negative atoms.
We will do an example: suppose that the $j$-th clause is
$x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}\vee \neg x_{i_2}$.
In addition to the classes $c_{i_0}$, $c_{i_1}$, and $c_{i_2}$ created earlier,
we also create three new classes $t_j$, $f_j$ and~$z_j$, with
capacity of one student each.
And we don't just create one student, we create three: $s_j$, $u_j$, and $v_j$.
Here are their lists.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|cc}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Student}}
    &$\ell$                        &$\hat{\ell}$  \\
  \hline
  $s_j$                  &$\set{c_{i_0},c_{i_1},t_j}$    &$\set{c_{i_2},f_j}$  \\
  $u_j$                  &$\set{t_j,z_j}$               &$\set{\text{F}}$  \\
  $v_j$                  &$\set{\text{T}}$              &$\set{f_j,z_j}$  
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

To confirm that this reduction function suffices,
we must show that if there is an assignment of truth values to the
Boolean variables that satisfies the formula then 
there is a course schedule solution, and we must also show the converse.

So first suppose that there a way to give each Boolean variable a value of
$T$ or $F$ such that the entire propositional logic formula evaluates
to~$T$.
Then we get a non-conflicting course time slot alottment by:
where $x_i=T$ put the course~$c_i$
in the same time slot as T,
and where $x_j=F$ put the course~$c_j$ in the F slot.
This clearly works for the all-positive atom or all-negative atoms cases.
The interesting cases are the mixed postive and negative atom clauses, such as
$x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}\vee \neg x_{i_2}$.
% For the input propositional logic formula to evaluate to $T$ this
% clause itself must evaluate to $T$.
We can check that for each possible assignment making this clause
evaluate to $T$ there is a non-conflicting way to arrange the other
courses.
For instance, suppose that
$x_{i_0}=T$, $x_{i_1}=F$, and $x_{i_2}=T$.
We have put course~$c_{i_0}$ in the time slot when~T is offered and
student~$s_j$ can take it then, and similarly can also take
course~$c_{i_2}$ when F is offered.
Then student~$u_j$ takes~$t_j$ when T is offered and also takes
course~F, and student~$v_j$ takes T and~$f_j$.

Conversely, suppose that for any assignment of truth values to
the Boolean variables the input formula is not satisfied.
We will show that implies that for any allocation of classes into time slots
there is a conflict.

So fix an allocation of classes into time slots, say with
the classes $c_{i_0}$, $c_{i_1}$, \ldots{} being offered in the
same slot as class~T and
the rest with class~F.
Associate with that allocation of classes the assignment of Boolean variables
that sets $x_{i_0}=T$, $x_{i_1}=T$, etc.
and the rest to~$F$.
This assignment causes the formula to yield a value of~$F$,
as this formula is not satisfiable.
So for this assignment the formula has some clause, clause~$j$, that does not
evaluate to~$T$.

The first possibility is that clause~$j$ has either all positive atoms
or all negative atoms.
An example is the clause $x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}\vee x_{i_2}$.
Then the assignment must have
$x_{i_0}=F$, $x_{i_1}=F$, and $x_{i_2}=F$, and so the allocation of classes
must be that all three of $c_{i_0}$, $c_{i_1}$, and $c_{i_2}$ go with class~F.
This will cause a conflict in course assignments because the output instance
was created with a student~$s_j$ having the lists
$\ell_{s_j}=\set{c_{i_0}, c_{i_1}, c_{i_2}}$ and $\hat{\ell}_{s_j}=\set{F}$.

The other case is that clause~$j$ has a mixed form, such as
$x_{i_0}\vee x_{i_1}\vee \neg x_{i_2}$, so that the Boolean variable
assignment is
$x_{i_0}=F$, $x_{i_1}=F$, and $x_{i_2}=T$.
This association comes from the allocation of classes that puts 
$c_{i_0}$ and $c_{i_1}$ with class~F, and puts $c_{i_2}$ with~T.
We claim that there is no non-conflicting way to assign students to courses.
Refer to the table above.
This allocation of classes could only be non-conflicting if student~$s_j$
selected classes~$t_j$ and~$f_j$.
But then because of the capacity of one in these classes,
student~$u_j$ must select~$z_j$ and~$F$, and student~$v_j$
has a conflict. 

Whew!
To close, observe that the reduction function that creates the
output $\prob$ instance from the input propositional logic formula
runs in polytime.
\end{example}

Another way to use the basic list
to show that a problem~$\prob$ is $\NP$~complete
is to find a special case of~$\prob$ on that list.

%<*ex:KnapsackIsNPComplete>
\begin{example}
\probname{Knapsack} starts with a 
multiset of objects~$S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$ where the
elements
have a weight~$w(s_i)\in\N^+$ and a value~$v(s_i)\in\N^+$, along with a
weight bound $B\in\N^+$ and value target~$T\in\N^+$.
It asks if there is
$K\subseteq S$ whose elements have total weight less than or equal to the
bound and total value greater than or equal to the target.

This is an $\NP$~problem.
The witness~$\omega$, where $\omega[i]=1$ if $s_i$ is in
the knapsack, and $\omega[i]=0$ if it is not, is only $k$~bits long and so is
$\bigOh(n)$ in the number of elements in $S$.
A deterministic machine can verify this witness in polynomial
time since it only has to total the weights and values
of the elements of~$K$.

To show that \probname{Knapsack} is $\NP$-hard, we show that a special case
is $\NP$-hard.
Consider the case where $w(s_i)=v(s_i)$ for all~$s_i\in S$, and where
$B$ and~$K$ each equal half of the total of all the weights.
This is a \probname{Partition} problem, which is in the above list.
\end{example}
%</ex:KnapsackIsNPComplete>

There are problems that are members of $\NP$ but are not
$\NP$~complete.
Most experts believe that \probname{Factoring} is hard for classical
computers\footnote{%
  In 1994, P~Shor discovered an algorithm for a quantum computer that
  solves the Factoring problem in polynomial time.
  This will have significant implications if quantum computation proves
  to be possible to engineer.}
but that it is not $\compclass{NP}$~complete.
Experts also suspect that \probname{Graph Isomorphism} is not $\NP$ complete.  
As always with such judgements, though, without proof they could be
mistaken.
\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete|)}





% ...................................
\subsection{$\EXP$}\index{EXP@$\EXP$|(}
In this chapter's first section we included in the list of
commonly encountered orders of growth $\bigOh(2^n)$
and~$\bigOh(3^n)$, and by extension other exponentials.

%<*def:EXP>
\begin{definition}
A problem is an element of the complexity class $\EXP$ if there is an
algorithm for solving it that runs in time $\bigOh(b^{p(n)})$ for some
constant base~$b$ and polynomial~$p$.
\end{definition}
%</def:EXP>

%<*discussion:EXP>
\probname{Satisfiability}\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem} 
can be solved in exponential time
by checking each row of the truth table, and any $\compclass{NP}$ problem
can be solved from \probname{Satisfiability} with only an addition of
polytime.
So $\EXP$ has this relationship to the classes we have already studied.
%</discussion:EXP>

%<*lem:PSubNPSubEXP>
\begin{lemma}
$\P\subseteq\NP\subseteq\EXP$
\end{lemma}
%</lem:PSubNPSubEXP>

%<*pf:PSubNPSubEXP>
\begin{proof}
Fix $\prob\in\NP$.
We can verify $\prob$ on a deterministic Turing machine~$\TM$
in polynomial time using a witness whose length is bounded by the
same polynomial.
Let this problem's bound be $n^c$. 

We will decide $\prob$ in exponential time by brute-forcing it:~we
will use~$\TM$ to run every possible verification. 
Trying any single witness requires polynomial time, $n^c$.
Witnesses are in binary so for length~$\ell$ there
are $\sum_{0\leq i\leq\ell}2^{i}=2^{\ell+1}-1$~many possibile ones;
In total then, brute force requires $\bigOh(n^c 2^{n^c})$ operations.
Finish by observing that $n^c 2^{n^c}$ is in $\bigOh(2^{n^c})$. 
\end{proof}
%</pf:PSubNPSubEXP>

We don't know whether there are any $\compclass{NP}$ problems that
absolutely require exponential time.
Conceivably $\compclass{NP}$ is contained in a smaller determinstic
time complexity class\Dash for instance, 
maybe \probname{Satisfiability}
can be solved in less than exponential time.
But we just don't know.

\begin{fig}[fig:BeanWithExp]{The bean encloses all problems.
   Shaded are the three classes $\P$, $\NP$, and $\EXP$, with
   $\EXP$'s outline highlighted.
   They are drawn with strict containment, which most experts guess
   is the true arrangement, but no one knows for sure.}
  \setlength{\unitlength}{1cm}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp07.pdf}}
\end{fig}

% In short, taking Cobham's Thesis, we
% can think that $\P$ stands not for `polynomial time' but instead
% for ``practical.''
% And, if $\NP=\EXP$, then we can take it to stand for
% ``not practical.''
Whereas a first take on 
polytime is ``can conceivably be used,'' 
a first approximation of
$\EXP$ is that for some of its 
problems the best algorithms are just too slow to imagine using.
% https://complexityzoo.uwaterloo.ca/Petting_Zoo
However, the big take-away from $\EXP$ 
is that it contains nearly every problem, and every class, that
we concern ourselves with in practice.
We can construct theories about still harder problems, but
$\EXP$ is big enough to contain most problems
we ever seriously hope to attack.
\index{EXP@$\EXP$|)}




% .................................
\subsection{$\P=\NP$?}\index{P versus NP@$\P$ versus $\NP$|(}
Every deterministic Turing machine is trivially a nondeterministic machine,
and so $\P\subseteq\NP$.
What about the other direction?

Posing the $\P$~versus~$\NP$ question asks:~does adding parallelism add speed?
Can unbounded parallelism bring problems
from super-polynomial to polynomial?

The short answer is that no one knows.
One of these two pictures is right but
we do not know which one.
\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeansPNP]{Which is it: $\P\subset\NP$
  or $\P=\NP$?}
  % \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  % \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp04.pdf}}
  \hspace{5em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp05.pdf}}
\end{fig}
\noindent There is a simple way
to settle the question.
By \cref{lem:InteractionNPCompleteAndReduction}, if someone shows
that any $\NP$ complete problem is a member of~$\P$ then
$\P=\NP$.
If someone shows that there is an $\NP$ problem that is not a member
of~$\P$ then $\P\neq\NP$.
But despite nearly a half century of effort by many extremely bright
people, no one has done either one.

Many people have worked on this question because this
is an extremely important problem.\footnote{%
  One indication of its importance is that it is on a list
  of problems in mathematics for which there is a one million dollar prize;
  see \protect\url{http://www.claymath.org/millennium-problems}.}
True, as formulated in Karp's original paper, the question of whether
$\P$ equals $\NP$ may seem of only technical interest.
\begin{quotation}
  \citetext{A large class}{%
  See \autocite{Karp}.}
  of computational problems involve the
  determination of properties of graphs, digraphs, integers,
  arrays of integers, finite families of finite sets, boolean formulas and
  elements of other countable domains.
  Through simple encodings \ldots{} these problems can be converted into
  language recognition problems, and we can inquire into their
  computational complexity.
  It is reasonable to consider such a problem satisfactorily solved when
  an algorithm for its solution is found which terminates within a
  number of steps bounded by a polymomial in the length of the input.
  We show that a large number of classic unsolved problems of covering,
  matching, packing, routing, assignment and sequencing are equivalent,
  in the sense that either each of them possesses a
  polynomial-bounded algorithm or none of them does.
\end{quotation}
But 
%<*discussion:PvsNPi>
Karp demonstrated that many of the problems people have been struggling
with in practical applications fall into this category.
Researchers who have been struggling with 
\probname{Vertex Cover} and those who have been
struggling with \probname{Clique}
found that they are in some sense working on the same problem.
By now the list of these problems includes
determining the best layout of transistors on a chip,
developing accurate financial-forecasting models,
analyzing protein-folding behavior in a cell,
or finding the most energy-efficient airplane wing.

In practice, proving that a problem is a member of $\NP$ is often an
ending point; a researcher may well reason that continuing to
try to find a algorithm is likely to be not fruitful since many of the
best minds of mathematics, science, and computer science have failed
at it.
They may instead turn their attention elsewhere, perhaps to
approximations.
%</discussion:PvsNPi>

In the book's first part we studied problems that are unsolvable.
That was a black and white situation; either a problem is mechanically solvable
in principle or it is not.
We here study that many problems of interest
are infeasible.
That is, 
the class of $\compclass{NP}$ complete problems form
a kind of transition between 
the possible and the impossible.

\margingraphic{0.40\textwidth}{complexity/xkcdnpcomplete.png}{\label{fig:xkcdBigO}\href{https://xkcd.com/287/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
We can use this to engineering advantage.
For instance, schemes for holding elections are notoriously prone to
manipulation and there are theorems that say they must be.
But we can hope to use system that, while in principle
is manipulatable, are constructed so that it is in practice infeasible to
compute how to do that.
Another example of the same thing is the celebrated
RSA encryption system that is used to protect Internet
commerce; see~\cref{extra:RSA}.  

This
returns us to the book's opening question about
mathematical proof.
%<*discussion:Entscheidungsproblemi>
Recall the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}} 
that was a motivation behind
the definition of a Turing machine.
% It was proposed in 1928 by Hilbert and Ackermann.
It asks for
an algorithm that inputs a mathematical statement~$\sigma$
and outputs whether $\sigma$ is true.
Maybe it is a \citetext{caricature}{%
  Paul Erdős joked that a mathematician is a machine for turning
  coffee into theorems.}
but imagine that the job of mathematicians is to prove theorems.
The \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} asks if we can replace them with machines.

In the intervening century we have come to understand, through
the work of G\"odel and others,
the difference between a statement's being true and its being provable,
and that we cannot always decide from axioms whether a statement is true.
Consequently, we modify
the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} to ask for an algorithm
that inputs statements and decides whether they are provable.

The plan is in principle simple.
A  proof is a sequence of statements,
$\sigma_0$, $\sigma_1$, \ldots, $\sigma_k$,
where the final statement is the conclusion,
and where each statement either is an axiom or
else follows from the statements before it
by an application
of a rule of logic (a typical rule allows the simultaneous replacement of all
$x$'s with $y+4$'s).
In principle a computer could brute-force the question of
whether a given statement is provable by
doing a dovetail, a breadth-first search of all derivations.
If a proof exists then it will appear eventually.
%</discussion:Entscheidungsproblemi>

%<*discussion:Entscheidungsproblemii>
The difficulty is that final word, eventually.
This algorithm is very slow.
So we ask:~Is there a tractable way?

In the terminology we now have,
the modified \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} is a decision problem:~given
a statement~$\sigma$ and bound~$B\in\N$, we ask if there is 
a sequence of statements witnessing a proof ending in~$\sigma$
that is shorter than the bound. 
A computer can quickly check whether a given proof is valid.
So with the current status of the
$\P$ versus~$\NP$ problem,
the answer to the question in the prior paragraph is that
no one knows of one but no one can show there isn't one either.
%</discussion:Entscheidungsproblemii>

%<*discussion:Entscheidungsproblemiii>
As far back as 1956, G\"odel\index{Godel, K@G\"odel, K!letter to von Neumann} 
raised these issues in a letter to
von~Neumann (this letter did not become public until years later).
\begin{quotation}\small
One can obviously easily construct a Turing machine,
which for every formula $F$ in first order predicate logic and every
natural number $n$, allows one to decide if there is a proof of $F$ of
length $n$ ($\text{length} = \text{number of symbols}$).
Let $\Psi (F,n)$ be the number of steps the machine requires for this
and let $\phi (n) = \max_F \Psi(F,n)$.
The question is how fast $\phi (n)$ grows for an optimal machine.
One can show that $\phi (n) \geq k \cdot n$.
If there really were a machine with
$\phi(n) \sim k \cdot n$
(or even $\sim k \cdot n^2$),
this would have consequences of the greatest importance.
Namely, it would obviously mean that in spite of the undecidability of
the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem}, the mental work of a mathematician
concerning Yes-or-No questions could be completely replaced by a machine.
After all, one would simply have to choose the natural number $n$ so large
that when the machine does not deliver a result,
it makes no sense to think more about the problem.
Now it seems to me, however, to be
\citetext{completely within the realm of possibility
  that $\phi (n)$ grows that slowly}{%
  Hartmanis observes in \autocite{Hartmanis} that it is interesting
  that G\"odel, the person who destroyed Hilbert's program of
  automating mathematics, seemed to think that these problems quite
  possibly are solvable in linear or quadratic time.}.
Since it seems that $\phi (n) \geq k \cdot n$ is the only estimation which
one can obtain by a generalization of the proof of the undecidability
of the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem} and after all
$\phi(n) \sim k \cdot n$ (or $\sim k \cdot n^2$)
only means that the number of steps as opposed to trial and error can be
reduced from $N$ to $\log N$ (or $(\log N)^2$).
% However, such strong reductions appear in other finite problems,
% for example in the computation of the quadratic residue symbol using repeated
% application of the law of reciprocity.
\ldots{}
It would be interesting to know, for instance,
the situation concerning the determination of primality of a number and
how strongly in general the number of steps in finite combinatorial problems
can be reduced with respect to simple exhaustive search.
\end{quotation}
%</discussion:Entscheidungsproblemiii>

\noindent
So we can compare $\P$~versus~$\NP$ with the \HP.
The \HP{} and related results tell us, in the light of
Church's Thesis, what is knowable in principle. 
The $\P$~versus~$\NP$ question, in contrast,  
speaks to what we can ever, in practice, know.



% .........................................
\subsection{Discussion} % https://www.scottaaronson.com/papers/pnp.pdf and https://arxiv.org/pdf/1108.1791.pdf
%<*discussion:PvsNPii>
The $\P$ versus~$\NP$ question 
is certainly the sexiest one in the Theoretical of Computing today.
It has attracted a great deal of speculation, and gossip.
\citetext{In 2018 a poll}{%
  The poll was conducted by 
  W~Gasarch is a prominent researcher and blogger in Computational Complexity.
  There were 124 respondents.
  For the description see
  \url{https://www.cs.umd.edu/users/gasarch/BLOGPAPERS/pollpaper3.pdf}.
  Note the suggestions that both respondents and even the surveyer
  took the enterprise in a light-hearted way.}
of experts found that out of 152 respondents, 
\citetext{$88\%$ thought that $\P\neq\NP$}{%
  Gasarch divided respondents into experts, 
  the people who are known to have seriously thought about the problem, and 
  the masses.
  The experts were $99\%$ for $\P\neq\NP$.} 
while only $12\%$ thought that $\P=\NP$.
%</discussion:PvsNPii>
This subsection discusses some of the intuition around the question. 

\margingraphic*{0.25\textwidth}{complexity/pix/jigsaw.png} % By Psyon (Own work) [CC BY-SA 3.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/b
%<*discussion:PvsNPiii>
First the intuition that $\P\neq\NP$.
When we work on a jigsaw puzzle we perceive that
checking that the solution is correct is very much easier than
finding the solution in the first place.
The checking is mechanical, tedious.
But the finding, we think, is creative\Dash solving
a jigsaw puzzle by brute-force trying every possible piece against every
other strikes us
as far too much computation to be practical.

Similarly, encryption schemes are engineered so that, given an
encrypted message, decrypting it with the key is fast and easy
but trying to brute force decrypt it by doing all possible keys is,
we think, just not tractable.

This is the distinction between problems in~$\P$
and problems that are $\NP$~complete.
A problem is in $\P$ if finding a solution is fast,
and a problem is in $\NP$ if verifying the correctness of a given
witness~$\omega$ is fast.
From this point of view, the result that $\P\subseteq\NP$
becomes the observation that if a problem is
fast to solve then it must be fast to verify.
But inclusion in the other direction seems to most experts to be unlikely.

Thus, informally. the $\P$ versus~$\NP$ question asks if 
finding a solution as fast as recognizing one.
If $\P=\NP$ then the two jobs are, essentially, equally difficult.
But to most people,
checking a proposed solution in polynomial time seems easier than
finding a solution in polynomial time.
These people guess that $\P\neq\NP$.
%</discussion:PvsNPiii>

% The collective failure by many talented people to find efficient algorithms for specific NP-complete problems strongly suggests that no efficient algorithm exists for any of them.

Many commentators,
\citetext{including Cook}{See \autocite{Cook}.},\cite{Cook, S}
have extended this thinking outside
the bounds of Theoretical Computer Science.
``Similar remarks apply
to diverse creative human endeavors,
such as designing airplane wings, creating
physical theories, or even composing music.
The question in each case is to what
extent an efficient algorithm for recognizing a good result can be found.''
Perhaps it is hyperbole to say that if $\P=\NP$ then writing
great symphonies would
be a job for computers, a job for mechanisms,
but it is correct to say that if $\P=\NP$ and if we can write
fast algorithms to recognize excellent music\Dash and our everyday
experience with Artificial Intelligence makes this seem more and
more likely\Dash 
then we could have fast mechanical writers of excellent music.

We finish with a taste of the contrarian view.

% \begin{TODO}
% Matching Problem (Marriage Problem).
% The naive algorithm tries all possible matches looking for one that
% is good.
% Even with only a hundred people, there are more things to try than
% there are atoms in the universe.
% But since the 1960's we have an algorithm (due to J~Edmonds) that
% does the job in polytime.
% That is, in this case we see a collapse from apparent exponential time to
% polynomial time.
% Why not look for it to happen every time?

% This coflates exponential with NP.
% Need to bring EXP in before this.
% \end{TODO}

%<*discussion:PvsNPiv>
\citetext{Many observers}{% 
  For example,  \autocite{Viola}} 
have noted that 
there are many cases where everyone ``knew'' that some algorithm was the 
fastest to solve
a certain problem but it proved not to be so.
The section on Big-$\bigOh$ begins with one, the grade school algorithm for
multiplication.
Another is the problem of solving systems
of linear equations.
The Gauss's Method algorithm is
obvious, and natural, and runs in time $\bigOh(n^3)$.\footnote{%
  Here is a small analogy for both the grade school multiplication and
  Gauss's Method results:
  consider the algebra problem of computing $ac+ad+bc+bd$.
  Someone might claim that to evaluate it ``obviously'' requires
  four multiplications.
  But then someone else counters that it equals $(a+b)(c+d)$,
  and so only requires one multiplication.
  The two cited results
  are a few among many examples evincing that naturalness and obviousness
  do not guarantee that the answer is correct, that no one will
  come along with a clever way that does the job with less.}
But while  trying to prove that Gauss's Method is optimal,
Strassen instead found
a method that is $\bigOh(n^{\lg 7})$ ($\lg 7\approx 2.81)$.
Every day  on the Theory of Computing blog feed
\autocite{blog:TOC} there are examples of this, of researchers producing
algorithms faster than the ones previously known.
R~J~Lipton expresses the same sense~\autocite{Lipton}.
\begin{quotation}
Since we are constantly discovering new ways to program our ``machines'',
why not a discovery that shows how to factor? or how to solve $\SAT$?
Why are we all so sure that there are no great new programming methods
still to be discovered?
\ldots{}
% Methods that could allow us to \ldots{} solve $\SAT$?
I am puzzled that so many are convinced that these problems could not
fall to new programming tricks, yet that is what is done each and every
day in their own research.
\end{quotation}
%</discussion:PvsNPiv>
%<*discussion:PvsNPv>
Knuth has a related but somewhat different take, \autocite{Knuth}.
\begin{quotation}
Some of my reasoning is admittedly naive: It's hard to believe that
$\P\neq\NP$ and that so many brilliant people have failed to discover why.
On the other hand if you imagine a number $M$ that's finite but incredibly
large \ldots{} then there's a humongous number of possible algorithms that
do $n^M$ bitwise or addition or shift operations on $n$ given bits,
and it's really hard to believe that all of those algorithms fail.

My main point, however, is that I don't believe that the equality
$\P=\NP$ will turn out to be helpful even if it is proved,
because such a proof will almost surely be nonconstructive.
Although I think $M$ probably exists, I also think human beings will never
know such a value.
I even suspect that nobody will even know an upper bound on $M$.

Mathematics is full of examples where something is proved to exist,
yet the proof tells us nothing about how to find it.
Knowledge of the mere existence of an algorithm is completely different
from the knowledge of an actual algorithm.
\end{quotation}
%</discussion:PvsNPv>

Of course, all this is speculation.
Speculating is fun but in the end these researchers, and all of us,
look to settle the question with proof.
\index{P versus NP@$\P$ versus $\NP$|)}



\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
You hear someone say, ``The $\probname{Satisfiability}$ problem is $\NP$ 
because it is not computable in polynomial time, so far as we know.''
It's a short sentence but find three things wrong with it.
\begin{answer}
The main thing wrong is that ``not computable in polynomial time'' 
isn't the criteria for membership in~$\NP$; rather the criteria is 
being verifiable in polytime.

Another thing wrong is that it seems to confuse $\NP$ with the 
set difference $\NP-\P$.
That is, the speaker seems to mean something like, ``experts guess that
$\probname{Satisfiability}$ is a problem in $\NP$ but not in~$\P$.''
Along with that, the speaker no doubt means $\NP$~complete, not just~$\NP$.

One more thing wrong is that the statement seems to say that the problem 
is in that class because there is not algorithm yet known.
It is not a question of whether there is a known algorithm, only of 
whether there is any algorithm at all.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false: $\NP$ is a subset of $\NP$~complete, which is a subset of
$\NP$-hard.
\begin{answer}
That ``$\NP$ is a subset of $\NP$~complete'' is false.
Rather, $\NP$~complete is a subset of~$\NP$.

The other one, that ``$\NP$~complete is a subset of $\NP$-hard'' is true.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % 
Suppose that the language $A$ is polynomial time reducible to the language~$B$,
$A\leq_p B$. 
Which of these are true?
\begin{exparts}
\item A tractable way to decide $A$ can be used to tractably decide~$B$.
\item If $A$ is tractably decidable then~$B$ is tractably decidable also.
\item If $A$ is not tractably decidable then $B$ is not tractably decidable too.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This is wrong.
For instance, if $A=\emptyset$ then $\emptyset\leq_p B$ for any language~$B$
but while there is a decider for $\emptyset$ that runs in time~$\bigOh(1)$, 
there may be no polynomial time decider for~$B$
(for instance, if $B=K$ then there is no decider at all).
The correct statement is the opposite:~a 
polytime decider for~$B$ can be used to decide 
the set~$A$ in polytime.
\item This is also wrong. 
As in the prior item, if $A$ is the empty set, which is decidable, 
and $B=K$, then $A\leq_pB$ but $B$ is not decidable in polytime.
The reason that $A\leq_pB$ is that $A$ can be decided in polytime
without any regerence to~$B$ at all; in fact, deciding $A$ is $\bigOh(1)$.
Instead, the other way around is right:~if 
$B$ is polytime decidable then $A$ is polytime decidable also.
\item This is true.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % http://www.cs.princeton.edu/courses/archive/fall02/cos126/exercises/np.html
Assume that $\P\neq \NP$.
Which of these can we infer from the fact that \probname{Primality} is in $\NP$
but is not known to be $\NP$-complete?
\begin{exparts}
\item
There exists an algorithm that solves arbitrary instances of 
\probname{Primality}.
\item
There exists an algorithm that efficiently solves arbitrary instances
of \probname{Primality}.
\item
If we found an efficient algorithm for \probname{Primality} then we could 
immediately use it to solve \probname{Travelling Salesman}.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % See http://www.cs.princeton.edu/courses/archive/fall02/cos126/exercises/np-sol.html
Suppose $\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$. 
For each, decide if it is true or false.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
If $\prob_0$ is $\NP$~complete then so is~$\prob_1$. 
\item
If $\prob_1$ is $\NP$~complete then so is~$\prob_0$. 
\item
If $\prob_0$ is $\NP$~complete and $\prob_1$ is in~$\NP$ then~$\prob_1$ is
$\NP$~complete. 
\item
If $\prob_1$ is $\NP$~complete and $\prob_0$ is in~$\NP$ then~$\prob_0$ is
$\NP$~complete. 
\item
It cannot be the case that both $\prob_0$ and~$\prob_1$ are $\NP$~complete
\item
If $\prob_1$ is in $\P$ then so is~$\prob_0$. 
\item
If $\prob_0$ is in $\P$ then so is~$\prob_1$. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
False
\item
False
\item
False
\item
True
\item
False
\item
False
\item
True
\end{exparts*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\probname{Vertex Cover} inputs a graph
$\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}$ and a number~$k\in\N$,
and asks if there is a subset~$S$ of at most $k$~vertices 
such that for each edge at least one endpoint is an element of~$S$.
The \probname{Independent Set} problem inputs a graph and a 
number~$\hat{k}\in\N$
and asks if there is a subset~$\hat{S}$ with at least $\hat{k}$~vertices 
such that for each edge at most one endpoint is in~$\hat{S}$.
The two are obviously related.
We will show both that $\text{Independent Set}\leq_p\text{Vertex Cover}$
and that $\text{Vertex Cover}\leq_p\text{Independent Set}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Consider this graph.
Find a vertex cover~$S$ with $k=4$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity22.pdf}
\end{center}
\item In that graph
find an independent set~$\hat{S}$ with $\hat{k}=6$ elements.
\item Prove that $S$ is a vertex cover if and only if
its complement~$\hat{S}=\mathcal{V}-S$ is an independent set.
\item Conclude that $\probname{Independent Set}\leq_p\probname{Vertex Cover}$
and that $\probname{Vertex Cover}\leq_p\probname{Independent Set}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item A vertex cover with $k=4$ elements is $S=\set{q_2,q_5,q_8,q_9}$.
\item An independent set with $\hat{k}=6$ elements is 
  $\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4,q_6,q_7}$.
\item For any edge, if it has at least one endpoint in
$S$ then it has at most one endpoint in the complement $\hat{S}=\mathcal{V}-S$.
Conversely, If an edge has at most one endpoint in~$\hat{S}$ 
then it has at least one endpoint in the complement~$S=\mathcal{V}-\hat{S}$. 
\item We will do $\probname{Independent Set}\leq_p\probname{Vertex Cover}$; the 
other reduction is similar.
The definition requires that we produce a polytime computable
function~$f$.
Given an instance of \probname{Independent Set} 
$\sigma=\sequence{\mathcal{G},\hat{k}}$,
then we convert that to an instance of  \probname{Vertex Cover}
$f(\sigma)=\sequence{\mathcal{G},\lh{\mathcal{G}}-\hat{k}}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Recall that \probname{Subset Sum} inputs a multiset~$T$ 
and a target~$B\in\N$,
and decides if there is a subset~$\hat{T}\subseteq T$ 
whose elements add to the target.
Recall also that \probname{Partition} inputs a multiset~$S$ and decides whether
or not it has a subset $\hat{S}\subset S$ so that the sum or elements
of~$\hat{S}$ equals the sum of elements not in that subset.
\begin{exparts}
\item Find a subset of $T=\set{3,4,6,7,12,13,19}$ that adds to $B=30$.
\item Find a partition of $S=\set{3,4,6,7,12,13,19}$.
\item Show that if the sum of the elements in a set is odd then the 
  set has no partition.
\item Express each problem as a language decision problem.
\item Prove that $\probname{Partition}\leq_p\probname{Subset Sum}$.
(\hint~handle separately the case where the sum of elements in~$S$ is odd.)
\item Conclude that \probname{Subset Sum} is $\NP$ complete.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The sum of elements in $\hat{S}=\set{6,7,19}$ equals the sum 
  of elements in $S-\hat{S}=\set{3,4,12,13}$.
\item The sum of the elements of $\hat{T}=\set{4,6,7,13}$ is $B=30$.
\item If we can partition a set $S\subset\N$ into 
$\hat{S}\subset S$ and $S-\hat{S}$
with equal sums, then the total of the elements of $S$ is twice the sum
of the elements of~$\hat{S}$, and so is even.
\item This is the Subset Sum problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{T,B}\suchthat
            \text{a subset~$\hat{T}\subseteq T$ has $\sum_{t\in\hat{T}}t=B$}}
\end{equation*}
This is the Partition problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{S}\suchthat
                \text{there is $\hat{S}\subset S$ with $\sum_{s\in\hat{S}}s=\sum_{s\in S-\hat{S}}s$}}
\end{equation*}
\item We must produce a 
polytime computable function that
takes in ~$\sigma=\sequence{S}$
and outputs $f(\sigma)=\sequence{T,B}$, such that
$\sigma\in\lang_0$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$. 

Given $\sigma=\sequence{S}$, consider the sum of its elements
$x=\sum_{s\in S}s$.
Where 
\begin{equation*}
  f(\sigma)=
  \begin{cases}
  \sequence{S,x/2}  &\case{if $x$ is even}   \\
  \sequence{S,x+1}  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
we have that $\sigma\in\lang_1$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$, as
required (and $f$ is clearly computable in polytime).
\item \probname{Partition} is one of the $\NP$~complete sets listed in the
section.
So \probname{Subset Sum} is $\NP$-hard.
But \probname{Subset Sum} is clearly~$\NP$; as a witness~$\omega$ we can use
the subset.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% Possible exercise: https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/109236

\begin{exercise} % https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/92028
If a problem~$\lang$ is in $\NP$ and $\lang\leq_p\hat{\lang}$
then what can we say about the problem~$\hat{\lang}$?
\begin{answer}
Nothing.
For instance, it could be that $\lang$ is in~$\P$ and so is $\hat{\lang}$.
It could also be that $\hat{\lang}$ is in some higher-up class.
There is not enough information to conclude anything. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  % https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/1810
Are there any known problems in $\NP$ and not in $\P$ that aren't $\NP$ 
complete?
\begin{answer}
No, if we knew of a problem in $\NP-\P$ then we'd know that $\P\neq\NP$.
We don't know that.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that $\leq_p$ is transitive: if $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_1$ 
and~$\lang_1\leq_p\lang_2$ then $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_2$.
\begin{answer}
Let $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_1$ via the function~$f$,
and let $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_2$ via the function~$g$,
which are polytime computable.

Then for all $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$ we have 
$\sigma\in\lang_0$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$,
and $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$ if and only if $\composed{g}{f}(\sigma)\in\lang_2$.
Because $f,g$ are polytime, $\composed{g}{f}$ is polytime also.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The difficulty in settling $\P=\NP$ is to get lower
bounds. 
That is, the trouble lies in showing that the given problem cannot be solved 
by any algorithm without such-and-such many steps. 
A common mistake is to think that any algorithm must visit all
of its input and then to produce a problem with lots of input.
Show that the successor function can be done 
on a Turing machine in constant time,
in only a few steps, so that the running time if the input is large is
the same as the time if the input is small.
That is, show that this problem can be done with an algorithm that does
not visit all the input:~on a 
Turing machine given input~$n$ in unary, 
with the head under the leftmost \str{1}, end
with $n+1$-many \str{1}'s, with the head under the leftmost~$1$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
This illustrates how large a problem can be and still be in $\EXP$.
Consider a game that has two possible moves at each step.
The game tree is binary.
\begin{exparts}
\item How many elementary particles are there in the universe?
\item At what level of the game tree will there be more possible branches
than there are elementary particles?
\item
Is that longer than a game can reasonably run, for instance a chess game?
(Chess has more than two moves at each step but it makes a reasonable
game comparison.)
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Googling around gives the number $10^{86}$.
\item $\lg(10^{86})=86\cdot\lg(10)\approx 86\cdot 3.32=285.52$
\item The average chess game is about 40 moves (see \autocite{SE:Chess}).
The longest game in the cited databasse is $277$ moves, so $286$
is a long chess game, but not completely out of range.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % http://www.cs.princeton.edu/courses/archive/fall02/cos126/exercises/np.html
\probname{Travelling Salesman} is $\NP$~complete.
From $\P\neq\NP$ which of the following statements could we infer?
\begin{exparts*}
\item
No algorithm solves all instances of
        \probname{Travelling Salesman}.
\item
No algorithm quickly solves all instances of
        \probname{Travelling Salesman}.
\item
\probname{Travelling Salesman} is in~$\P$.
\item
All algorithms for \probname{Travelling Salesman}  run in polynomial time.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts*}
\item False; for example, the brute force algorithm solves all instances.
\item True, where we use Cobham's Thesis to take `quickly' to mean polytime.
\item False.
\item False.
\end{exparts*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  % http://www.cs.princeton.edu/courses/archive/fall02/cos126/exercises/np.html
Suppose that $\prob_1\leq_p \prob_0$.
Determine if each statement is correct.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
If $\prob_0$ is $\NP$ complete then so is $\prob_1$.
\item
If $\prob_1$ is $\NP$~complete then so is $\prob_0$.
\item
If $\prob_0$ is $\NP$~complete and $\prob_1\in\NP$ then $\prob_1$
is also $\NP$~complete.
\item
If $\prob_1$ is $\NP$~complete and $\prob_0\in\NP$ then $\prob_0$
is also $\NP$~complete.
\item
 Not both of $\prob_0,\prob_1$ are $\NP$~complete.
\item
If $\prob_1$ is in~$\P$ then $\prob_0$ is in~$\P$.
\item
If $\prob_0$ is in~$\P$ then $\prob_1$ is in~$\P$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item No.
\item No.  It might not be a member of $\NP$.
\item No.
\item Yes.
\item No.
\item No.
\item Yes.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Which of these are $\NP$~complete?
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The brute force algorithm for solving \probname{Satisfiability}.
\item
The quicksort algorithm for sorting strings.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
This is a trick question.
Computational classes such as~$\NP$ do not contain algorithms, they
contain problems.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that if $\P=\NP$ then every $\mathcal{P}\in\P$ is
$\NP$~complete, except for the problems of determining
membership in the empty language and the full language,
$\mathcal{P}=\emptyset$ and $\mathcal{P}=\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
\end{exercise}
